Quick review · SAA-C03

S3 server access logs/CloudTrail/Storage Lens/analytics answers different questions: which requests, who operates them, capacity and usage trends.

S3 access control, sharing & access points

Purpose: Grant permissions to the correct bucket/object and share private files without opening all data publicly.

Permission check flow:

Client request
↓ IAM / bucket / access point policies
S3
↓ allowed
Object

1. IAM policy, bucket policy and ACL

IAM policy grants permissions to users/roles; bucket policy grants permissions from the resource side, useful for cross-account or AWS services.

Explicit Deny still beats Allow; SSE-KMS adds a key permission layer.

Bucket ARN is used for bucket level actions like ListBucket; object ARN is used for
GetObject/PutObject.
Do not confuse arn:aws:s3:::bucket with arn:aws:s3:::bucket/*.

S3 ACL — permissions on bucket or object

S3 ACL (Access Control List) grant basic permissions to AWS accounts or supported groups on buckets or individual objects; Does not have the same level of detail/conditions as IAM and bucket policies.

TypeScope and examples
Bucket ACLPermissions at the bucket level: READ allows listing objects; Do not allow yourself to read the content of every object.
Object ACLPermissions on each object: READ to read file; READ_ACP/WRITE_ACP to read/edit ACLs.
READ = list bucket or read object, depending on ACL type
WRITE = create/delete object at bucket level; Does not apply to Object ACLs
READ_ACP = read ACL
WRITE_ACP = edit ACL
FULL_CONTROL = full ACL permissions applicable to that resource

For example: Account A owns report.pdf and grant read permission to Account B via Object ACL when ACL is on.

AWS Account A
└── S3 Bucket
    ├── Bucket ACL → bucket grant permission
    └── report.pdf
        └── Object ACL → Account B: READ

ACL issued to account B through canonical user ID; B needs to allow his IAM principal to use the granted permissions. Explicit Deny and KMS permissions (if any) are still checked.

The new bucket defaults to using Object Ownership = Bucket ownership effective: ACL is disabled, permissions are managed by policy. Usually keep this default and use bucket/IAM policy for cross-account. 

Block Public Access
= guardrail prevents unwanted public opening; A public Allow is not necessarily effective
force if guardrail blocks.

2. Pre-signed URL

The backend signs the URL for an action/key for a limited time.

The person with the URL uses signer rights for that request, without needing to receive an access key; The URL is a bearer token so it must be kept private.

The URL does not override the signer's authority and may expire early when the temporary credential expires.

With SigV4 and IAM User credential, the maximum period is usually 7 days; Don't consider every URL to live 7 days.

Pre-signed URLs are not disposable by default. Upload browser needs proper CORS in addition to PutObject permission.

3. Access Points

Each Access Point has its own hostname and policy for the application/team, pointing to the same
a bucket.
Bucket policy and access point policy must coordinate; Access Points are not bypassed
bucket guardrail.

VPC-only Access Point limits access through the specified VPC, combining VPC Endpoint and endpoint policy.

Having a route to S3 still requires full IAM/bucket/access point/KMS permissions.

Multi-Region Access Point (MRAP)

MRAP provides a global endpoint for S3 buckets in many Regions. The request itself goes through the AWS network to the bucket with the lowest network latency among the available buckets active routing status; Integrated automatic MRAP AWS Global Accelerator.

             Global users
                   |
                   v
          MRAP Global Endpoint
                   |
           +-------+-------+
           |               |
           etc
       S3 Bucket A S3 Bucket B
       US Region Singapore
           |               |
           +----- CRR -----+
             Copy data

MRAP routes requests; CRR copies data. Cross-Region Replication must be configured separately between buckets; If receiving records in both Regions, two-way replication configuration is appropriate. CRR is asynchronous, so data does not appear immediately in the other bucket.

MRAP does not select a bucket based on whether the object exists or not: GET can return 404 If the selected bucket does not have an object. For example, US and Singapore users use the same endpoint to read replicated data in two Regions. ·

4. S3 Object Lambda

Object Lambda uses Lambda to change the content when passing through the Object Lambda Access Point, for example hiding PII or changing format; The original object is not modified.

From November 7, 2025, the feature is only available to existing customers and certain qualified partners.

Keep conceptual to understand old SAA documents; when the new design can be processed via Lambda/API Gateway/CloudFront or before saving. AWS availability change.

5. Use cases

Use cases: Download temporary private file: pre-signed GET. Browser uploads directly: pre-signed PUT. Data lake with multiple teams: Access Points and policy by prefix. Cross-account: Allows both the IAM side and the appropriate resource/key.

6. S3 ACL Details & Maximum Presigned URL Term

S3 decentralization mechanismScope & Limitations to remember
Bucket owner effective (Default)The new bucket completely disables ACLs by default; Only re-enable ACLs when you really need to authorize individual objects
Bucket ACL vs Object ACLBucket ACL control list/write permissions at bucket level; Object ACL Grants permission to read each individual object (no permission to edit object content via Object ACL)
S3 Presigned URL (SigV4)Creation via SDK/CLI has a maximum duration 7 days (or shorter if signed with STS temporary credentials about to expire)
Quick review · SAA-C03

Bucket policy, IAM policy, Access Points, and CloudFront OAC address different access layers; S3 Block Public Access is an important public blocking layer.

CloudFront & Global Accelerator

Amazon CloudFront: CDN & origins

Purpose: Deliver web/files via edge near users to reduce latency and backend load.

1. Cache at the edge

Viewer → edge location
          ├─ cache hit → return content
          └─ cache miss → origin → cache when policy allows → return content

Origin: where the original content is located.

Distribution has one or more origins; cache behavior determines origin and policy for each URL path.

CloudFront serves both static and dynamic HTTP(S), not just static files. Uncache requests can still use network and connection optimization.

CloudFront and origin do not need to be in the same AZ. CloudFront is a global service running in edge locations; origin can be in an AWS Region or on-premises. VPC origin must use supported Region and resources.

2. Origins

Origin / mechanismFunctionFor example
S3 OriginGet content from S3 bucketHTML, CSS, JS, images
Custom OriginGet content from HTTP/HTTPS serverPublic ALB, EC2, web server on-premises
VPC OriginPrivate connections to VPC resources are supportedInternal ALB, NLB, EC2
Origin GroupPrimary + secondary origins group for failoverPrimary ALB fails → Secondary S3 serves the backup site

Origin Group is a backup group, not a type of server. Failover according to configured connection error/status codes, GET/HEAD/OPTIONS only; OPTIONS must also be enabled in cached HTTP methods. Do not use this mechanism to failover POST yourself.

VPC origin helps origin to be private, but must still meet SG/network requirements and supported features.

Public custom origins need to limit users from bypassing CloudFront if edge protection is required.

3. Cache Behaviors select origin

Path patternOriginRequest example
/images/*S3/images/logo.png
/api/*ALB/api/orders
Default (*)On-premises server/home

CloudFront reviews behaviors in the configured order and use the first matching behavior; Do not choose the most specific pattern yourself. If individual behavior does not match, use Default Cache Behavior (*).

4. Private S3 and OAC

OAC signs request from CloudFront, bucket policy for distribution is read; No need to open a public bucket. Object SSE-KMS also needs a suitable KMS key policy.

S3 REST bucket origin is different from S3 website endpoint. The website endpoint is a custom HTTP origin, not using OAC in the way of a private bucket origin.

5. TLS and edge protection

Custom domains need a certificate with the correct name.

ACM certificate for viewer → CloudFront must be in us-east-1; certificate for origin is a separate leg. AWS CloudFront certificate requirements.

WAF filters application requests; Shield helps protect against DDoS; signed URL/cookie controls viewer access to private content.

OAC grants CloudFront permission to read S3, without authenticating each user.

6. Use cases

Use cases: Global website/photo/video: CloudFront + S3. Private app needs edge delivery: VPC origin is suitable. HTTP(S) API: behavior/cache policy according to content and security requirements.

7. CloudFront Video Streaming (VOD vs Live) & Custom Domain HTTPS

Streaming typeStandard architecture on AWSCache mechanism on CloudFront
Video On-Demand (VOD)S3 (Origin) → CloudFront → UsersDirectly cache static video files/segments stored on S3
Live StreamingCamera → Elemental MediaLive (encode) → MediaPackage (segments/playlist) → CloudFrontCloudFront does not encode but cache each created segment immediately (manifest keeps TTL short)
Custom Domain HTTPS on CloudFront:
1. Apply for Public Certificate in ACM at Region us-east-1
2. Declare Alternate Domain Name (CNAME) on CloudFront + attach ACM certificate
3. Create Route 53 A/AAAA Alias pointing the domain name to CloudFront Distribution
Quick review · SAA-C03

CloudFront is a user-friendly CDN cache, distributed from origins. S3 origin and custom origin have different origin protection policies.

CloudFront caching, delivery controls & geo restriction

Purpose: Cache the right content, update it at the right time and limit who can receive the content.

Cache stream:

Viewer requests
↓ Cache keys
CloudFront edge
├─ hit → cached response
└─ miss → origin → response / cache according to policy

1. Cache key

Cache key determines which requests share the same response. Path is the main part; You can add query strings, headers/cookies as different content.

Putting too many variables in the key reduces cache hits.

Leaving out variables that actually change the content may return erroneous data; Individual responses according to users need safe or non-cache policies.

2. Cache policy and origin request policy

Cache policy determines key/TTL. Origin request policy transfers additional information to origin even though it is not in the key.

Forward header/cookie does not by itself guarantee a unique response. Check cache behavior with origin's Cache-Control, especially minimum TTL and sensitive data.

3. TTL, file version and invalidation

TTL regulates cache time.
Origin changes the file without automatically making every edge get a new version immediately.
Use a version name like app.abc123.js for assets that don't change; HTML can have short TTL
more.

Invalidation removes path from cache before TTL: /index.html, /images/* or /*.

Wildcard path helps delete many objects but invalidation still has quota/cost; It is not a mechanism to backup or repair the original file.

4. Signed URL and signed cookie

Signed URL matches a specific file; signed cookie merges multiple files such as videos
many segments.
Both control the duration/conditions of private content access via CloudFront.

Block direct access to the origin if you want every user to pass this check. S3 pre-signed URL is another mechanism, granting requests directly to S3 with signer permissions.

5. Geo restriction

Allowlist/blocklist by country in distribution based on Geo-IP.
VPN/proxy may affect location identification; do not change
authentication/authorization by user.

6. Price Class

Price Class Choose a group of edge locations serving distribution to balance costs and latency.

Price ClassScopeTarget
100America, Canada, Europe, IsraelLimited to lower cost areas
200Class 100 + many regions in Asia, Africa, Middle East; There is VietnamBalance cost and coverage
AllAll CloudFront edge locationsLargest global coverage

For example, customers in Vietnam: Class 100 does not include edge in Vietnam, so you may have to go further; Class 200 includes Vietnam so latency can be reduced; All opens the entire edge network. Actual costs depend on traffic and service area.

Price Class does not block users by location. People outside the range can still access it; If you want to restrict countries, use Geo Restriction/WAF.

7. Viewer Protocol Policy

PolicyMeaning
HTTP and HTTPSAllows both protocols
Redirect HTTP to HTTPSConvert HTTP viewer to HTTPS; HTTP/1.0 requests using write methods may be rejected
HTTPS OnlyOnly for HTTPS; HTTP returns 403

Policy applies to the leg viewer → CloudFront; The CloudFront → origin route has its own protocol policy.

8. Use cases

Use cases: JS/CSS versioned: Long TTL. Quickly changed HTML: invalidation. Paid content per file: signed URL; multiple video segments: signed cookie. The content varies by query: the cache key contains the necessary query.

9. Automatic Compression (Gzip/Brotli) & 4 Viewer/Origin Events hook points

Automatic Compression: when the browser sends Accept-Encoding: br, gzip, CloudFront automatically compresses eligible files using Brotli/Gzip at Edge, compressed and paid cache Content-Encoding.

Event HookRunning timeSupported by
Viewer Request / Viewer ResponseEvery request from the user to Edge and before returning (even when Cache HIT)CloudFront Functions & Lambda@Edge
Origin Request / Origin ResponseOnly runs when Cache MISS and Edge must call OriginOnly Lambda@Edge
Quick review · SAA-C03

Cache key/TTL affects hit ratio; Behaviors and origin request policies control what content/headers/cookies are sent and cached.

AWS Global Accelerator

Purpose: Bring TCP/UDP traffic into the AWS network near the client, keep the IP stable and transfer it to a healthy endpoint.

1. Anycast and network line

Anycast
= multiple network points broadcast the same IP; The client goes to the appropriate edge and then traffic
follow the AWS backbone to the endpoint.
Client → static Anycast IP → AWS edge → AWS backbone → regional endpoint

Global Accelerator IPv4 typically provides two static IPv4; dual-stack adds IPv6. IP stays stable when changing backend, useful for firewall allowlist.

2. Endpoints and health

Endpoint group by Region contains ALB, NLB, EC2 or EIP according to the supported configuration.
Health controls where traffic is received; Endpoint weight and traffic dial adjustable
distribution.
Traffic dial changes the rate of traffic entering a Region; endpoint weight divided in
group. group.
When there is an error, the service changes network routing without having to wait for the client to run out of DNS cache
get a new IP.

3. No cache

Global Accelerator accelerates the network, does not cache HTTP objects.
New content is still coming to the app; TLS, authentication and request control remain
needed at the endpoint/application.

Do not confuse the Global Accelerator's static IP with the AZ static IP of an NLB: Accelerator sends global traffic to regional endpoints.

4. Use cases

Use cases: UDP game, VoIP, global TCP app; Multi-Region APIs need stable IP allowlists and quick transitions from faulty endpoints. Website needs to cache images/videos near the user: consider CloudFront.

5. Target Endpoint types supported by Global Accelerator

Global Accelerator routes to all Internet-facing ALB/NLB, Internal ALB/NLB, EC2 Instances and Elastic IP in one or more Regions without exposing the internal network structure.

Quick review · SAA-C03

Global Accelerator uses static anycast IP and the global AWS network to improve access to regional endpoints; Does not cache content like CloudFront.

CloudFront vs Global Accelerator vs S3 CRR

Purpose: Distinguish between content acceleration, network acceleration and persistent data replication.

Three mechanisms:

CloudFront = HTTP(S) delivery + cache
Global Accelerator = TCP/UDP acceleration, no cache
S3 CRR = copy object to another Region

1. Comparison table

MechanismWhat to do?When choosing
CloudFrontCDN for HTTP(S), caches at the edge and passes requests to the originWeb/API, photos, videos, signed content
Global AcceleratorTCP/UDP over AWS backbone with static Anycast IP, no cacheGlobal app, IP allowlist, network failover
S3 CRRReplicate object to bucket in another Region, asynchronouslyDR, compliance, data must have a regional copy

2. Cache is different from replica

CloudFront cache may evict or run out of TTL; origin is still the data source.

CRR creates a real object in the destination bucket, requires Versioning and permission; The app that wants to read the target version must access the correct bucket/endpoint.

Do not use CloudFront cache instead of backup/DR for S3. CRR does not automatically distribute HTTP to the edge or automatically move all clients to a backup bucket.

3. CloudFront is different from Accelerator

CloudFront understands HTTP and has cache behavior, signed URL/cookie, geo restriction.
Global Accelerator handles TCP/UDP and static IP routes; no key cache/TTL
like CDN.

HTTP app can also use Accelerator when a fixed IP/network failover is needed. Choose according to the main requirement, not just based on the word “global”.

4. Use cases

Use cases: Photo website: CloudFront. Multi-Region UDP Game: Global Accelerator. Object needs another Region copy: CRR. It's possible to have CRR for DR and CloudFront for delivery, but you still have to configure origin/failover accordingly.

Quick review · SAA-C03

CloudFront optimizes HTTP content caching; Global Accelerator optimizes network path/TCP-UDP; S3 CRR copies data between Regions.

AWS Storage Extras

AWS Snow Family

Purpose: Understand how to transfer data using a physical device when transferring over the network takes too long or the workplace lacks connectivity.

1. Why use equipment?

Hundreds of TB via slow transmission can take weeks/months. Snowball Edge copies data on-premises and then transports the device to AWS for import into S3.

Create job → receive device → copy data → send back to AWS → import to S3

Compare network transmission time with receive/copy/send/import time; Actual bandwidth, cost and deadline must also be considered.

2. Snowball Edge and edge compute

Storage Optimized prioritizes capacity; Compute Optimized prioritizes processing at the place of data creation.

Edge compute integrates data filtering, inference or media processing at low-Internet sites before sending data to the cloud.

Data on the device is encrypted; Key management, job credentials and shipping are still important parts of the process.

3. Import and archive

Import into S3 and then use Lifecycle to switch to the appropriate Glacier layer, do not choose the "Snowball import directly into Glacier vault" route.

Do not use the device for continuous synchronization that requires frequent updates; online recurring copy is more suitable for DataSync.

4. Note current availability

From November 7, 2025, Snowball Edge is only available to existing customers.

The Snowcone/Snowmobile in the old document is not the new option that is always available.

Keep the concept of offline migration for learning, but when implementing, check the service/solution currently provided. AWS Snowball availability.

5. Use cases

Use cases: Data center has a lot of data and limited WAN: offline bulk transfer if eligible. Site disconnected needs to be handled locally: edge compute. The NAS has a good enough connection and needs scheduled copying: DataSync.

Quick review · SAA-C03

Snow Family uses large-scale data movement/processing when network lines are not suitable; Select device according to data size and edge compute request.

Amazon FSx managed file systems

Purpose: Choose a managed file system with protocols and features suitable for the existing application.

Types of file systems: FSx for Windows File Server · FSx for Lustre · FSx for NetApp ONTAP · FSx for OpenZFS

1. FSx menu

TypeProtocol/strengthsSuitable
Windows File ServerSMB, NTFS, Active DirectoryWindows share, home directory, app Windows
LustreParallel file system, S3 integrationHPC, ML training, media processing
NetApp ONTAPNFS/SMB/iSCSI, ONTAP featureNAS/NetApp migration, multi-protocol
OpenZFSNFS, snapshot/clone/compression ZFSLinux/ZFS workload needs low latency

2. Windows File Server

Integrated AD, file permissions/ACL and user quotas. Linux clients can also mount SMB; This is not an EC2 Windows-only service.

Multi-AZ deployment increases BP; Backup protects data.
On-prem access via VPN/DX with appropriate route, SG and file permissions.
Windows SMB shares often choose Windows FSx instead of EFS NFS.

3. Luster and S3

Luster helps multiple compute nodes process files in parallel with high throughput. You can link datasets in S3, read them as files and export the results to S3.

Scratch is for temporary processing, not replicating data to protect file server errors like Persistent. Persistent combines data that needs to be kept and longer workloads. Choose deployment according to durability and supported features, don't assume every FSx has the same HA model.

4. ONTAP and OpenZFS

ONTAP is suitable for applications that rely on NetApp, multiple protocols, snapshots/clones and tiering. OpenZFS is suitable for ZFS/NFS applications and corresponding snapshot/clone features.

Quick Clone does not replace independent backup. Encrypt at rest using KMS; encryption in transit and file permissions depend on protocol/filesystem.

5. Use cases

Use cases: Company Windows share + AD: FSx Windows. HPC reads dataset S3: Luster. Lift-and-shift NetApp NAS: ONTAP. Workload is using ZFS: OpenZFS. Shared Linux files across multiple AZs: compare EFS first.

6. Integrate S3, Single-AZ/Multi-AZ & Storage tier of 4 FSx series

FSx seriesSingle-AZ / Multi-AZStorage optionsHow to integrate S3
FSx for Windows File ServerBothSSD / HDDCopy data over AWS DataSync
FSx for LustreSingle-AZ (file server)SSD / HDD / Intelligent-TieringOriginal link Data Repository Association (DRA) Automatically import metadata & export HPC results to S3
FSx for NetApp ONTAPBothSSD + Capacity Pool (Auto Tiering)Mount S3 Access Points (read/write via S3 API while data remains on FSx)
FSx for OpenZFSBothSSD / Intelligent-TieringMount S3 Access Points or switch to DataSync

FSx for Lustre Intelligent-Tiering Automatically transfer data through 3 layers: Frequent Access (< 30 days) → Infrequent Access (≥ 30 days) → Archive Instant Access (≥ 90 days); This mode's data has multi-AZ persistence but components Luster file server still runs Single-AZ.

Quick review · SAA-C03

FSx for Windows = SMB/AD; FSx for Luster = HPC; FSx for NetApp ONTAP and OpenZFS serve other file system needs.

AWS Storage Gateway

Purpose: For on-prem applications to use the familiar file/block/tape protocol with storage in AWS.

1. Gateway and local cache

Gateway: appliance connects on-premises applications to the cloud.

Local cache holds recently used data for quick access; Data is transmitted to AWS according to the gateway mechanism.

Storage Gateway serves seamless hybrid access. It is different from DataSync which runs a transfer job and Snowball which transfers data by device.

2. S3 File Gateway

App on-prem --NFS/SMB--> File Gateway + cache --HTTPS--> S3 objects

The app sees the file share, S3 saves the object. Gateway has IAM role to access bucket; SMBs can use AD. Share/file permissions and S3 permissions are classes that need to be configured.

Do not consider the local cache as a permanent place for all data or that the gateway file is a shared POSIX filesystem like EFS.

3. Volume Gateway

Provide block volumes via iSCSI. Cached volumes keep the main data in AWS, caching the portion in use locally to reduce local storage. Stored volumes keep all core data on-premises and backed up to AWS.

Backup uses EBS snapshots to serve restore.

Stored is suitable when you need to access the entire dataset with local latency; cached is suitable when you want to reduce on-prem capacity.

4. Tape Gateway

Provides iSCSI virtual tape library (VTL) for existing backup software. Tape stored on AWS, can be archived to Glacier Flexible Retrieval/Deep Archive according to configuration.

No more buying and shipping physical tape; Restoring tape archives still has retrieval time/costs to consider.

5. Use cases

Use cases: NFS/SMB share backed by S3: File Gateway. Block storage on-prem with cloud backup: Volume Gateway. Replace the tape library but keep the backup software: Tape Gateway.

6. Compare Volume Gateway: Cached Volumes vs Stored Volumes

CriteriaCached VolumesStored Volumes
Where full original data is keptAbove Amazon S3 (managed by Storage Gateway)Right now On-premises to access the entire dataset locally
On-premises disk space requiredShow Cache (recently read data) + Upload BufferEntire volume capacity + Upload Buffer
Backups on AWSVolume + EBS Snapshots scheduledBackup asynchronously to S3 as EBS Snapshots
Quick review · SAA-C03

Storage Gateway connects on-prem applications to AWS storage using a compatible file/volume/tape protocol.

AWS DataSync & Transfer Family

Purpose: Distinguish between copying data between the storage system and the endpoint receiving files from users/partners.

1. DataSync

DataSync runs the task of transferring data between supported storage such as NFS/SMB/HDFS/object storage and S3/EFS/FSx. There is scheduling, data checking, bandwidth limit and transmission over TLS.

NAS on-prem → DataSync agent/task → S3 / EFS / FSx
On-prem file storage often requires an agent; AWS-to-AWS in the same regular account
no agent needed.
Some cloud/cross-account sources have different rules by location and task mode.

2. Metadata and sync

It is possible to keep permissions/metadata supported in source/destination pairs. Copying NFS to S3 does not turn S3 into a POSIX filesystem with full file permissions as the source.

This is a transfer task that runs on schedule or on demand, not CDC database or real-time filesystem replication. AWS metadata behavior.

3. Transfer Family

Managed endpoint for clients to use SFTP/FTPS/FTP to transfer files to S3/EFS according to supported protocols; AS2 serves B2B exchanges with its own support backend.

Partners using SFTP client → Transfer Family → S3 prefix are authorized
Authentication can be service-managed, AD or custom provider depending on the protocol.
IAM role/policy and filesystem permissions limit where each user can access.
FTP is unencrypted and only uses the VPC endpoint; Do not choose FTP for sensitive files
via the Internet.

4. Select by task

RequestSelect
Copy large NAS to AWS, with schedule/verificationDataSync
Partners still use SFTP client to send filesTransfer Family
On-premises apps continue to use hybrid file/block/tapeStorage Gateway
Just need a private network lineVPN/Direct Connect; Also need transfer tool

5. Use cases

Use cases: Migration NFS → EFS: DataSync. Scheduled file synchronization: DataSync. Instead of self-managed SFTP server, data goes to S3: Transfer Family.

6. Transfer Family: SFTP Server vs Connector & Identity Providers

ComponentThe active party opens the connectionData stream
Transfer Family Server (SFTP Server)External partner clients connect to your EndpointClient actively Uploads or Downloads files from S3 / EFS
Transfer Family Connector (SFTP Connector)AWS is proactive Connect to the partner's external SFTP ServerExecute command SEND (S3 → partner) or RETRIEVE (partner → S3)
Identity Provider on Transfer FamilyUser authentication mechanism
Service-managed usersTransfer Family stores username + directly SSH Public Key and private S3/EFS directory mapping
AWS Managed Microsoft AD / AD ConnectorSign in with a business Active Directory account
Custom IdP (Lambda / API Gateway)Integrates external identity store, supports custom password + SSH key
Quick review · SAA-C03

DataSync transfers data automatically between storage systems; Transfer Family provides SFTP/FTPS/FTP protocols to AWS storage.

AWS storage selection guide

Purpose: Choose storage based on how the application accesses data first, then consider durability, latency and cost.

Select according to how the application reads/writes:

Block → EBS
Shared File → EFS / FSx
Object → S3
Local temporary disk → Instance Store

1. Block, file or object?

DemandService
Object/API, data lake, static assets, backupS3
Disk for EC2, boot or databaseEBS
NFS/POSIX files are shared by many Linux clientsEFS
SMB/AD or specialized file systemFSx
Local disk is very fast, data can be recreatedInstance Store

Do not choose S3 as disk mount for databases that need block I/O. Don't choose EBS so that multiple apps across AZs can share files. Archive object: select the S3 Glacier class according to the required retrieval time.

2. Where is the data located and can it be lost?

EBS is in one AZ, if you want to go to another AZ, you need a snapshot/restore.
Regional EFS across multiple AZs; One Zone reduces fees but accepts AZ risk.
Instance Store loses data over the instance/host life cycle.
FSx has different deployments; Check HA/backup of specific type.
Backup prevents accidental deletion; replication/failover serves availability, not replacement
replace each other.

3. Hybrid access or migration?

TaskService
Continue file/block/tape at on-prem, AWS backendStorage Gateway
Online bulk copy/sync has verificationDataSync
Receive SFTP/FTPS files from partnersTransfer Family
Offline bulk transferSnowball/physical transfer solutions are also available

4. Use cases

Use cases: Windows shared drive: FSx Windows. WordPress multiple EC2: EFS. Separate DB Disk: EBS io2/gp3 by I/O. Data lake: S3. Before closing, check RPO/RTO, throughput, encryption and transfer/retrieval cost.

Quick review · SAA-C03

Block storage → EBS; shared NFS → EFS; Specialized SMB/FS → FSx; object/backup → S3; Data migration → DataSync/Snow.

SQS, SNS, Kinesis & Amazon MQ

Amazon SQS

Purpose: Use managed message queue to:

Decouple producers and consumers · Asynchronous processing · Buffer traffic spike · Retry workload when consumer fails

Basic flow:

Producer
↓ SendMessage
SQS Queue
↓ ReceiveMessage
Consumer
↓ processing
DeleteMessage

1. Why do we need SQS?

If the producer calls the consumer directly:

Producer
↓
Consumer

Consumer is slow/error → producer is also affected.

With SQS:

Producer
↓
SQS Queue
↓
Consumer

SQS keeps messages temporarily so: Producer scale independent · Consumer scale independent · Traffic spike is buffered

For example:

Web App
↓ 1,000 video jobs
SQS
↓
Workers handle it gradually

SQS = decouple + async + buffer.

2. Message Lifecycle

Producer
↓ SendMessage
SQS
↓
Consumer ReceiveMessage
↓
Process
↓
DeleteMessage

ReceiveMessage ≠ delete message

The message is only deleted when the consumer calls:

DeleteMessage

If the consumer crashes before deletion:

Message
↓
Visibility Timeout is over
↓
Visible again
↓
Another consumer retry

3. Visibility Timeout

After the consumer receives the message:

Message
↓ Receive
Temporarily invisible
↓
Consumer processing

Default according to slide: 30 seconds

Configurable to: 12 hours

If processing is complete:

DeleteMessage
↓
Messages disappeared

If not delete:

Visibility Timeout is over
↓
The message reappears
↓
Retry

Points to remember:

Timeout is too short
→ duplicate processing

Timeout is too long
→ slow retry when consumer crashes

Can be changed with: ChangeMessageVisibility

Visibility Timeout = time the message is hidden while being processed.

4. Dead-Letter Queue (DLQ)

If an error message persists:

Receive
↓ fail
Retry
↓ fail
Retry
↓
maxReceiveCount
↓
DLQ

DLQ is used to hold: Poison messages · Messages error processing multiple times

→ help debug or reprocess later.

Main Queue
↓ fail many times
DLQ

5. SQS Standard Queue

Standard is the default choice when strict ordering is not needed.

Standard = very high throughput = at-least-once delivery = best-effort ordering

At-least-once means: A message can be delivered more than once

Therefore consumers must: Idempotent

Order ID = 123
↓ 1st processing
↓ duplicate
second processing

Consumers should check: Has Order 123 been processed yet?

to avoid charging/paying twice.

6. SQS FIFO Queue

FIFO is used when needed: Strict ordering + Deduplication

Queue name must: *.fifo

1
2
3
↓
FIFO Queue
↓
1
2
3

Ordering is guaranteed in: MessageGroupId

customer-A
↓
1 → 2 → 3

While:

customer-B
↓
1 → 2 → 3

Two different groups can process in parallel.

Multiple MessageGroupIds = increase parallelism

If there is only 1 group:

every message
↓
sequential processing

→ lower throughput.

7. FIFO Deduplication

FIFO supports: MessageDeduplicationId

or: Content-Based Deduplication

to avoid sending the same message multiple times in the deduplication window.

FIFO deduplication ≠ business exactly-once processing

Consumers should still design idempotent.

8. Standard vs FIFO

StandardFIFO
ThroughputVery highLower/limited
DeliveryAt-least-onceDeduplication helps avoid duplicate sends
OrderingBest-effortStrict in MessageGroupId
Use caseJob processing, async workloadsOrder/customer workflow requires order

Quick select:

No need for strict order
↓
Standard
Need to be in the correct order according to customer/order
↓
FIFO

9. Message Size & Retention

Maximum messages: 1 MiB

If payload is larger:

Large payload
↓
S3
↓
SQS only sends S3 reference

Retention:

Default
= 4 days

Configurable
= 1 minute → 14 days

Messages exist until: DeleteMessage · or · Retention exhausted

10. Long Polling

Consumer calls: ReceiveMessage

If using short polling:

No messages
↓
Return empty response immediately

Long polling:

ReceiveMessage
↓
SQS waits for messages
↓ maximum 20 seconds
Message arrives → pay immediately

Advantages: Fewer empty responses · Fewer API calls · Reduced polling costs

Frequently used: WaitTimeSeconds = 20

11. Scaling Consumers

Queue can be a buffer between:

Web Tier
↓
SQS
↓
Processing Tier

Consumers can be: EC2 · Auto Scaling Group · Lambda

When backlog increases:

Queue depth increased
↓
CloudWatch
↓
Scale out consumers

Commonly used metrics: ApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible

Better yet, you can use: Backlog per instance = Queue depth / number of consumers

→ Producer and consumer do not need to scale together.

12. SQS + Lambda

Popular flows:

Producer
↓
SQS
↓
Lambda
↓
Process messages

Lambda will scale consumers based on the backlog.

SQS helps: Buffer burst · Retry failed processing · Decouple producer from Lambda

13. Security

13.1. In Transit

HTTPS / TLS

13.2. At Rest

Can be used: SSE-SQS

SSE-KMS

If using KMS customer managed key:

Principal
↓
Needs appropriate KMS permissions

14. IAM and Queue Policy

IAM Policy controls: sqs:SendMessage · sqs:ReceiveMessage · sqs:DeleteMessage

Queue Policy is: Resource Policy · directly attached to SQS Queue

Often used for:

Cross-account
SNS → SQS
S3 → SQS
SNS Topic
↓
Queue Policy allowed
↓
SQS Queue

Can be limited by: aws:SourceArn

15. SQS vs SNS

Points that are easy to confuse: SQS = Queue = a message processed by a consumer

SNS = Pub/Sub = one fan-out message to many subscribers

If multiple systems need the same event:

Producer
↓
SNS Topic
├─ SQS Queue A
├─ SQS Queue B
└─ SQS Queue C

→ Each system has its own queue.

16. Overall flow

Producer
↓
SendMessage
↓
SQS Queue
↓
Consumer ReceiveMessage
↓
Visibility Timeout
↓
Process
↓
DeleteMessage

If error:

No Delete
↓
Message is visible again
↓
Retry
↓
maxReceiveCount
↓
DLQ

17. When to use SQS?

Use cases:

Decouple application components
↓
SQS
Traffic spike / burst
↓
SQS buffer
Async background jobs
↓
SQS
Strict ordering by customer/order
↓
FIFO
Quick review · SAA-C03

SQS decouple producer/consumer. Standard = at-least-once, best-effort ordering; FIFO = ordering in MessageGroupId and deduplication support.

Amazon SNS

Purpose: Using pub/sub push format for a publisher to send the same event to many subscribers.

Publisher
↓ Publish
SNS Topic
├─ SQS
├─ Lambda
├─ Email / SMS
└─ HTTP(S)

SNS = Pub/Sub + Push + Fan-out.

1. Topic, Publisher & Subscriber

Producers just need to publish to: SNS Topic

SNS will push messages to appropriate subscriptions.

Publisher
↓
SNS Topic
↓
Subscribers

Popular subscribers: SQS · Lambda · Amazon Data Firehose · HTTP / HTTPS · Email · SMS · Mobile Push

→ Publisher does not need to know how many consumers are behind it.

2. SNS Fan-out

A message can be sent to multiple systems:

Order Created
↓
SNS Topic
├─ Fraud Queue
├─ Shipping Queue
└─ Analytics Queue

Each subscriber receives a copy of event.

This is called: Fan-out

3. SNS + SQS

Pattern is very important:

Producer
↓
SNS Topic
├─ SQS Queue A
├─ SQS Queue B
└─ SQS Queue C

SNS: = fan-out event

SQS: = keep message = retry = buffer = consumer processes at its own pace

For example:

Order Created
↓
SNS
├─ Fraud SQS
├─ Payment SQS
└─ Shipping SQS

→ Each system is independent, a slow consumer does not affect other consumers.

Queue Policy must allow:

SNS Topic
↓ sqs:SendMessage
SQS Queue

4. Subscription Filter Policy

Default:

There is no Filter Policy
↓
subscriber receives every message

Filter Policy available:

SNS Topic
↓
Filter Policy
↓
Only suitable messages
↓
Subscriber
OrderStatus = Placed
↓
Shipping Queue
OrderStatus = Cancelled
↓
Cancellation Queue

→ No need to create multiple SNS topics just to separate message types.

5. AWS Services → SNS

Many AWS services can send notifications to SNS.

Important examples: CloudWatch Alarm · S3 Event · Auto Scaling · AWS Budgets · Lambda · RDS Events

CloudWatch Alarm
↓
SNS Topic
↓
Email / SMS / Lambda

6. SNS Security

6.1. In Transit

HTTPS / TLS

6.2. At Rest

Can be encrypted with: SSE-KMS

6.3. Access Control

IAM Policy = who called the SNS API

SNS Topic Policy = resource policy of Topic

Topic Policy is often used for:

Cross-account

or

AWS Service
↓
publish to SNS
S3
↓ Topic Policy allowed
SNS Topic

If using customer managed KMS key:

Publisher / AWS Service
↓
Needs appropriate KMS permissions

7. SNS FIFO Topic

SNS also supports FIFO when needed: Ordering + Deduplication

Ordering is based on: MessageGroupId

Deduplication uses: MessageDeduplicationId

or: Content-Based Deduplication

Pattern when needing fan-out but still maintaining order:

Producer
↓
SNS FIFO Topic
↓
SQS FIFO Queue
↓
Consumer

→ suitable when needed: Fan-out + Ordering + Deduplication

8. SNS vs SQS

Extremely confusing point:

SNS
= Pub/Sub
= push
= 1 message → many subscribers

SQS = Queue = Consumer poll = buffer / retry

1 event
↓
Many systems need to receive
↓
SNS
1 job
↓
Need worker to handle async
↓
SQS

If both are needed:

SNS
↓ fan-out
multiple SQS queues
↓
Each consumer processes independently

9. S3 Fan-out Pattern

If an S3 event needs to trigger multiple pipelines:

S3 ObjectCreated
↓
SNS Topic
├─ SQS Queue A
├─ SQS Queue B
└─ Lambda

→ publish once but many systems receive the event.

10. Overall flow

Publisher / AWS Service
↓
SNS Topic
↓
Subscription Filter
↓
SQS / Lambda / Firehose
HTTP(S) / Email / SMS

11. When to use SNS?

Use cases:

An event
↓
many consumers need to receive
↓
SNS
Notification
Email / SMS / HTTP
↓
SNS
Fan-out to multiple queues
↓
SNS + SQS
Fan-out + strict ordering
↓
SNS FIFO + SQS FIFO
Quick review · SAA-C03

SNS is pub/sub fan-out, pushing to many subscribers; SQS is a queue for consumer pull/private processing. Combine SNS → SQS when you need sustainable fan-out.

Amazon Kinesis Data Streams

Purpose: Collect and save real-time streaming data for multiple consumers to process independently.

Producers
↓
Kinesis Data Stream
↓
Shards
↓
Consumers

KDS is suitable when needed: Real-time streaming · High-volume events · Ordering · Multiple consumers · Replay data

1. Data flow

Producers can be: Application · IoT Device · Clickstream · Logs / Metrics · Kinesis Agent

Consumers are usually: Lambda · Application / KCL · Amazon Data Firehose · Apache Flink

Producer
↓
Partition Key
↓
Kinesis Stream
↓
Shard
↓
Consumer

2. Shard & Partition Key

Kinesis Stream is divided into: Shards

Each record has: Partition Key

Kinesis hash partition key to decide which shard the record goes into.

Record
↓
Partition Key
↓ hash
Shard

For example:

customer-123
↓
Shard A

Records with the same partition key:

customer-123
Event 1
Event 2
Event 3
↓
same shard
↓
keep the correct order

Ordering = ensure in the same partition key / shard ≠ entire stream

3. Hot Shard

If partition keys are unevenly distributed:

Many records
↓
same Partition Key
↓
with Shard
↓
Hot Shard

Bad example: Partition Key = "default"

→ Almost all records are put into one shard.

You should choose a well-distributed key such as: Customer ID · Device ID · User ID

4. Retention & Replay

KDS does not delete records immediately after the consumer reads them.

Record
↓
Kinesis Stream
↓
kept in retention period

According to the slides:

24 hours
→
365 days

During that time:

Consumer A → read
Consumer B → read
Consumer C → replay

→ multiple consumers can process the same data independently.

Other points of SQS: SQS = message is processed by consumer and then deleted

Kinesis = record still exists in retention = can be replayed

5. Capacity Modes

There are 2 modes: Provisioned · On-Demand

5.1. Provisioned

You manage the shard number.

Each shard follows the slide: Write = 1 MB/s or 1,000 records/s

Read = 2 MB/s

If traffic increases:

Traffic increased
↓
add shards

→ suitable when the throughput is relatively predictable.

5.2. On-Demand

Does not self-manage shard numbers
↓
Kinesis self-scales capacity

→ suitable for workloads that are difficult to predict or want to reduce capacity management.

Throughput/pricing details may vary by AWS; remember priority Provisioned = shard management, On-Demand = AWS scales itself.

6. Consumer Throughput

There are 2 main ways of reading.

6.1. Shared / Standard Consumer

Shard
↓
many consumers
↓
share read throughput

Multiple consumers reading at the same time will share the capacity of the shard.

6.2. Enhanced Fan-Out

Shard
├─ Consumer A → 2 MB/s
├─ Consumer B → 2 MB/s
└─ Consumer C → 2 MB/s

Each registered consumer has: 2 MB/s / shard / consumer

→ suitable when many consumers need to read the stream with high throughput and low latency.

7. KPL & KCL

7.1. KPL

Kinesis Producer Library = supports producers in sending records effectively

7.2. KCL

Kinesis Client Library = supports consumers reading shards + coordinating multiple workers + checkpoint

Checkpoint Help consumers remember: Which record has been processed?

to continue processing after restart/failure.

8. Data Size & Storage

According to slide v48: Record max = 10 MiB

KDS is suitable for: Multiple streaming records · sent continuously

KDS only keeps data during the retention period:

Kinesis = temporary streaming storage ≠ long-term data lake

If you need to save long term:

Kinesis
↓
Firehose / Consumer
↓
S3

9. Security

9.1. In Transit

HTTPS / TLS

9.2. At Rest

KMS Encryption

Access control: IAM · Resource Policy

Can be used: VPC Endpoint

Traffic to Kinesis does not need to go through the public Internet.

10. Kinesis vs SQS

Mistaken points: SQS = Queue = async job processing = consumer processes then deletes

Kinesis = Stream = real-time data = many consumers read the same data = has retention + replay

Video processing job
↓
SQS
Clickstream / IoT / logs real-time
↓
Kinesis

11. Overall flow

Producers
↓
Partition Key
↓
Kinesis Data Stream
↓
Shards
↓
Lambda / KCL / Flink / Firehose
↓
Real-time Processing / Analytics / S3

12. When to use KDS?

Use cases:

Website clickstream
↓
Kinesis
IoT telemetry
↓
Kinesis
Logs / metrics real-time
↓
Kinesis
One stream
↓
many independent consumers
↓
Kinesis
Need to replay old data
↓
Kinesis
Quick review · SAA-C03

Kinesis Data Streams holds the stream for multiple consumers to process/replay according to retention; Different from SQS because stream data is not deleted when a consumer reads it.

Amazon Data Firehose

Purpose: Bring streaming data to storage/analytics destination using managed service, not self-managing shard and consumer fleet.

1. Delivery pipeline

Producer / Kinesis Data Streams / logs
 → Firehose → buffer / optional transform → destination + backup S3 when configuring

Destination includes S3, Redshift, OpenSearch and HTTP/vendor supported. Delivery Redshift uses S3 as staging before COPY.

2. Buffer and latency

Firehose often collects data by size/time and then delivers it; suitable for near real-time ingestion, not having to default to logic that must respond to each event instantaneously.

There is a configuration to reduce/zero buffering at some destinations, but does not make Firehose a replayable stream for many consumers.

Service self-managed delivery capacity; There are still quotas and limits based on source/destination.

3. Transform and format conversion

Lambda can transform records before delivery.

Format conversion native converts JSON to Parquet/ORC for S3, using Glue Data Catalog schema; CSV needs to be transformed to JSON first, not directly converting all formats.

Compression/columnar format helps reduce storage and scan data when querying. AWS Firehose format conversion.

4. Replay, backup and permissions

Firehose does not provide retention streams for consumers to freely replay like Kinesis Data Streams does. If multiple consumers/replays are needed, put KDS first and Firehose as a consumer delivery.

There are retry and S3 backup for all/failed records depending on destination/configuration.

Service role needs source, destination, Lambda and KMS permissions; Design error monitoring and reprocess backup data.

5. Use cases

Use cases: Logs/clickstream → S3 data lake; data → Redshift/OpenSearch; Lambda transform → JSON → Parquet. Need to process real-time multiple consumers and read back events: Kinesis Data Streams.

Quick review · SAA-C03

Data Firehose is managed to deliver stream data to storage/analysis destinations; Does not require consumer self-management by shard like Data Streams.

Amazon MQ

Purpose: Use managed message broker for legacy applications that need traditional messaging protocols/APIs.

Amazon MQ = managed ActiveMQ / RabbitMQ = keep old protocol = lift-and-shift compatible

1. When to use Amazon MQ?

Use cases:

SQS/SNS uses AWS's own API.

But the old app might be using: AMQP · MQTT · STOMP · OpenWire · WSS · JMS

If rewriting to SQS/SNS is too expensive:

Existing Application
↓ keep current protocol
Amazon MQ

→ no need to change the entire messaging code.

2. ActiveMQ & RabbitMQ

Amazon MQ supports: ActiveMQ · RabbitMQ

Clients still use: Native Library · Native Protocol

Broker can provide: Queue · ≈ same as SQS · Topic · ≈ same as SNS

but still retains the routing, acknowledgment and semantics of ActiveMQ/RabbitMQ.

3. Amazon MQ vs SQS / SNS

Amazon MQ = traditional message broker = protocol compatibility = runs on broker instances

SQS / SNS = AWS-native messaging = serverless / managed scale is better

Legacy app needs AMQP/JMS/MQTT
↓
Amazon MQ
New AWS-native app
↓
SQS/SNS preferred

Amazon MQ does not scale nearly infinitely like SQS/SNS.

You still have to pay attention to: Broker size · Storage · Connections · Throughput · Queue depth

4. High Availability

Amazon MQ can deploy multiple AZ brokers depending on engine/configuration.

For example:

AZ A · Active Broker · AZ B · Standby Broker

When active broker fails:

Active Broker fail
↓
Standby Broker
↓
Client reconnect

→ increase High Availability.

According to the ActiveMQ architecture in the slide:

Active / Standby
↓
Shared EFS Storage

5. Network & Security

Broker runs in: VPC

Security Group controls:

Producer
Consumer
↓
broker connected or not

Commonly used authentication: Broker credentials · Native broker authorization

IAM is mainly used to manage: Create / Delete / Configure broker

You don't have to replace the broker credential for the data plane.

Encryption: In Transit = TLS

At Rest = KMS-backed encryption

6. When to use?

Old Java / JMS / ActiveMQ app
↓
Amazon MQ
RabbitMQ app needs
exchange / binding / routing
↓
Amazon MQ
Hybrid app uses
AMQP / MQTT / STOMP
↓
Amazon MQ

7. Overall flow

Existing Producer
↓
AMQP / MQTT / STOMP / JMS...
↓
Amazon MQ Broker
↓
Queue / Topic
↓
Consumer

If the broker fails:

Active Broker
↓ fail
Standby Broker
↓
Client reconnect
Quick review · SAA-C03

Amazon MQ helps migrate applications using traditional protocols/brokers to AWS; unlike SNS/SQS is an AWS-native messaging service.

SQS vs SNS vs Kinesis vs Firehose vs MQ

Purpose: Select messaging based on data reception, replay and application compatibility requirements.

1. Key comparison

ServiceModelDifferences
SQSQueue, workers pull/divide workBuffer job, retry/DLQ; message is deleted after processing
SNSPub/sub pushFan-out event to many subscribers; filter each subscription
Kinesis Data StreamsStream has retentionMany consumers read/replay; ordering by shard/partition key
Data FirehoseManaged deliveryBuffer/transform sends to destination, does not replay stream arbitrarily
Amazon MQManaged ActiveMQ/RabbitMQ brokerCompatible with existing protocols and broker applications

2. Queue another stream

SQS workers often share message processing; delete message when finished.

Kinesis keeps records in the retention window even after a consumer reads, allowing multiple consumers to read independently or replay.

SQS Standard has at-least-once/best-effort order; FIFO uses message group/deduplication to support consistent ordering.

Kinesis orders by shard, not a global order across all shards.

3. SNS + SQS

An event → SNS ┬→ Queue A → order processing
               └→ Queue B → analytics

Each system has its own queue, retry and scale.

SNS push to queue; worker pulls from SQS.

SNS does not have to replace every queue; archive/replay of SNS FIFO is a separate feature, it does not mean every SNS topic is streamed like KDS.

4. Kinesis + Firehose and MQ

KDS keeps the stream for analytics/replay; Firehose pushes a branch to S3/warehouse.
As long as managed delivery is needed, you can use Firehose directly without adding KDS.
MQ compatible migration app needs JMS/AMQP/MQTT/STOMP or semantics broker
engine. engine.
Broker has finite capacity and needs to choose HA deployment; new app can be used
SQS/SNS as appropriate.

5. Use cases

Use cases: Background jobs: SQS. One event to many systems: SNS + SQS. Telemetry multiple consumers/replay: KDS. Logs to data lake: Firehose. Legacy broker app needs few code changes: MQ.

6. Limit Payload, FIFO Deduplication & SNS → Lambda Resource Policy

ServiceMaximum sizeAnti-infection Mechanism & Authorization
DynamoDB Item400 KB (including attribute names and values; large file saved to S3 + holds S3 key in DynamoDB)Use Conditional Writes when idempotency is needed
SQS Message1 MiB (larger payload using S3 Extended Client)SQS FIFO filter accordingly MessageDeduplicationId or hash the content in the window 5 minutes
SNS Message256 KiB default (supports scaling up to 1 MiB / Extended Client with S3)SNS FIFO filter in 5 minutes just before fan-out to SQS FIFO subscribers; when SNS calls Lambda is required Lambda Resource Policy allow lambda:InvokeFunction with Principal: sns.amazonaws.com attached SourceArn
Quick review · SAA-C03

SQS = queue; SNS = fan-out; Kinesis Streams = streaming/replay; Firehose = delivery pipeline; MQ = traditional message broker.

ECS, Fargate, ECR & EKS

Amazon ECS – Orchestration, tasks, and services

Purpose: Amazon ECS is AWS's container orchestration service. You declare the container you need to run, its quantity and resources; ECS takes care of deploying, monitoring and replacing tasks when they fail.

Container Image
↓
ECS
↓
EC2 or Fargate

1. 3 main concepts

ECS conceptNature
Task DefinitionThe blueprint declares the container image, CPU/RAM, ports, environment variables and IAM Roles
TaskAn actual running instance is instantiated from the Task Definition
ServiceThe dispatcher maintains the correct quantity desiredCount Tasks are always running and attached to the Load Balancer

For example:

Service desiredCount = 3
↓
Task 1
Task 2
Task 3

A Task dies → ECS creates a new Task to replace it.

2. ECS Cluster

Cluster is Logical group contains Service, Task and compute capacity.

ECS Cluster
├── Service A
│   ├── Task
│   └── Task
└── Service B
    └── Task

Cluster is not a server.

3. EC2 vs Fargate

ECS + EC2 = EC2 administrator = instance type + patch + ASG + capacity

ECS + Fargate = AWS manages the server = you only choose CPU/RAM for the Task

Remember:

EC2
→ more controls
→ suitable for stable workload / need to optimize cost

Fargate
→ less infrastructure management
→ suitable for fluctuating workloads

ECS Anywhere and External launch type

ECS Anywhere Allows registering servers/VMs on-premises or outside of AWS to the ECS cluster to manage containers using ECS.

External launch type (EXTERNAL) used to run ECS tasks/services on these external instances; You still manage the server, OS and capacity.

Server/VM outside of AWS
↓ install ECS Agent + SSM Agent, register cluster
ECS Cluster
↓ launch type: EXTERNAL
Container Tasks on servers outside of AWS

For example, container management processes data at the factory from the ECS while the data remains on-premises.

4. Container flow

ECR Image
↓
Task Definition
↓
ECS Service / Run Task
↓
EC2 or Fargate
↓
Containers run

Service Used for apps to run continuously.

Run Task Used for jobs that run once.

5. IAM Roles

These two roles are easy to confuse:

Task Execution Role
= ECS is used to start Task
→ pull image from ECR
→ send CloudWatch Logs
→ get Secret
Task Role
= code inside the usage container
→ read S3
→ call DynamoDB
→ send SQS

If you run ECS on EC2, you also have: Container Instance Role = ECS Agent permissions on EC2

Execution Role = ECS used. Task Role = application used.

6. Load Balancer

  • awsvpc: Each task has its own ENI and SG; Available for EC2 tasks and required for Fargate. EC2 bridge/host mode uses the instance's networking.
  • Service registers task to target group. EC2 bridge mode can use dynamic host port mapping; awsvpc uses target type IP. .

ECS Service is usually placed as follows: ALB = HTTP / HTTPS = path / host routing

NLB = TCP / UDP = high performance

Client
↓
ALB
├── /users  → User Service
└── /orders → Order Service

7. Auto Scaling

Scaling mechanism on ECSHow it works
Service Auto ScalingApplication Auto Scaling increases/decreases desiredCount by min/max (Target Tracking by CPU, RAM, ALBRequestCountPerTarget; Step Scaling; Scheduled Scaling)
Cluster Auto Scaling (EC2)Capacity Provider connects ECS Cluster with ASG to automatically add EC2 when Pending Tasks lack space (need to attach capacity provider strategy)
targetCapacitySet 100% to take full advantage of EC2 or lower to leave headroom; ASG still needs a valid min/max
ECS on FargateAWS takes care of the entire underlying compute infrastructure; You only configure Service Auto Scaling for the number of Tasks

8. Storage

If multiple Tasks need to share the same file:

ECS Tasks
↓
Amazon EFS
↓
Shared Persistent Storage

S3: = Object Storage = accessible via SDK/API

Not a regular shared filesystem.

9. Use Cases

Use cases: 

ALB
↓
ECS
↓
Web API / Microservices
SQS
↓
ECS Worker
↓
Background Job
ECS Tasks
↓
EFS
↓
Shared Files

10. ECS Compute Modes, Task Placement Strategies & Scheduled Tasks

ECS Compute modeWho manages the Server?Distinguishing point
ECS on FargateAWS completelyServerless per Task (vCPU/RAM), no EC2 management
ECS Managed InstancesAWS operates EC2AWS takes care of provisioning/patching/scaling EC2 but supports choosing specialized machine models & GPUs
ECS on EC2You manage yourselfFull control over AMI, host OS, Capacity Provider attached to ASG
ECS AnywhereYou (machine outside of AWS)Register server/VM On-premises to ECS Cluster via EXTERNAL launch type
Task Placement Strategy (ECS on EC2)How to arrange TasksTarget
spreadSpread Tasks evenly across Availability Zones or EC2 instancesHigh Availability
binpackTightly place Tasks into the fewest EC2 instances according to CPU or MemoryOptimize costs, easily recover idle machines
randomRandomly select EC2 that meets the resource conditionsSimple allocation without priority

ECS Scheduled Task: run the container periodically according to cron/rate using Amazon EventBridge Scheduler call RunTask.

Quick review · SAA-C03

ECS Task Definition describes the container; Task is the run version; Service maintains task number. Task Role for app, Execution Role for ECS agent/Fargate.

AWS Fargate

Purpose: AWS Fargate is serverless compute for containers — used with ECS or EKS. You declare the CPU/RAM of task/pod; AWS provision, patch and operate the entire platform server.

Container run flow:

Container image + CPU/RAM
↓ ECS Task / EKS Pod
Fargate
↓
AWS operates compute

1. What is Fargate not?

🧠 Fargate Do not replace ECS/EKS. ECS/EKS is orchestration; Fargate just is launch/compute type — how you run the container, not the orchestration system.

2. Fargate vs EC2 launch type

CriteriaFargateEC2
Server managementAWS takes care of everythingYou manage
Charge unitCPU/RAM of taskInstance hours
Infrastructure controlLow (no SSH)High (SSH, custom)
Appropriate workloadSmall, irregular, serverlessLarge, stable, cost-optimized

3. What does Fargate still need?

VPC + Subnet — each task has its own ENI. Security Group — controls traffic in/out of tasks. IAM role — task execution role + task role. Image — from ECR or other registry. Logs — CloudWatch Logs (via execution role).

4. Fargate + EFS

Fargate task can be mounted Amazon EFS to have persistent shared storage for serverless containers — multiple multi-AZ tasks read/write the same file system.

5. Limits to remember

CPU/RAM must be a supported pair. For example, task 4 vCPU uses 8–30 GB of RAM; This is not Fargate's maximum level. .

Cannot SSH into the underlying infrastructure.

Some workloads need host-level control (eg custom kernel, GPU) → must use EC2 launch type.

6. Use cases

Use cases: 

APIs/microservices run on ECS that the team wants to focus on containers, no
Manage EC2 nodes.
Job on demand: run tasks when there is work; Use EFS if many tasks need it
persistent shared files.

7. Principles for calculating Compute fees on Fargate

Fargate only charges for compute vCPU and Memory allocated during the time the Task/Pod actually runs (don't pay for idle EC2); Expanded storage, data transfer and Load Balancer are charged separately.

Quick review · SAA-C03

Fargate runs containers without needing to manage EC2 hosts; Still have to configure CPU/RAM, network, IAM and scaling of workload.

Amazon ECR

Purpose: Amazon ECR (Elastic Container Registry) is a private Docker/OCI image registry managed by AWS — where container images for ECS, Fargate, or EKS are stored.

1. Typical usage flow

CI build image → push ECR (tag/digest) → ECS/EKS pull → container runs

2. Main point

Image is referenced by tag (vd :latest) or digest (sha256 — immutable, more secure).

Task execution role (ECS/Fargate) needs permissions ecr:GetAuthorizationToken + ecr:BatchGetImage to pull images.

Repository policy Together with IAM, you can control who can push/pull, especially with cross-accounts. Pull errors can also be due to missing NAT routes or ECR VPC endpoints.

Image scanning Vulnerability scanning (basic scan or Enhanced Scanning with Inspector).

Lifecycle policy manually clean up old images (e.g. keep the 5 newest images, delete the rest) — save costs.

Can replicate images to another Region/account; production should pin immutable tag or digest instead of dependency :latest.

Encrypt at rest: AWS managed key or KMS CMK.

3. Security

ClassMechanism
Control planeIAM (create/edit/delete repository)
Data planeRepository policy + IAM (push/pull)
At restSSE with AWS managed key or KMS CMK
In transitTLS (HTTPS)

4. Use cases

Use cases:

CI build/push image into ECR; ECS/EKS pulls according to digest to deploy the correct version.

Repository cross-account with IAM/repository policy; Scan for vulnerabilities and use lifecycle policy to clean up old images.

5. ECR Basic Scanning vs Enhanced Scanning

ECR scan modeScanning Range & Timing
Basic ScanningScan for basic OS packages vulnerabilities when pushing images or upon manual request
Enhanced ScanningIntegration Amazon Inspector to continuously and automatically scan both OS packages and programming language libraries as soon as new CVEs appear
Quick review · SAA-C03

ECR saves container images; ECS/EKS/Fargate uses images to run workloads. Distinguish between repository image and runtime container.

Amazon EKS

Purpose: Amazon EKS (Elastic Kubernetes Service) is a Kubernetes control plane managed by AWS — for teams that need Kubernetes APIs, ecosystem (Helm, operators), and portability.

1. Architecture

AWS-managed control plane (API server · etcd · scheduler)
↓
Workload: EC2 node groups or Fargate pods

2. Three ways to run workloads on EKS

WayDescriptionWhen to use
Managed node groupsAWS manages your ASG+ node (EC2) for youDefault, simplest
Self-managed nodesYou create/manage EC2 nodes yourselfNeeds maximum control
Fargate profilesPod runs on Fargate (serverless)Don't want to manage the node

3. IAM and Kubernetes RBAC

🧠 IAM access does not automatically grant Kubernetes permissions. IAM allows you to call the EKS API (create cluster, describe…); but the permissions in the cluster (get pods, deploy...) are due Kubernetes RBAC decide. Need to map IAM principal → Kubernetes role.

4. EKS vs ECS

CriteriaEKSECS
PlatformKubernetes (open-source, portable)AWS-native, proprietary
ComplexityHigher (learn Kubernetes)Lower, easy to start
EcosystemHelm, operators, tooling K8sLimited to AWS
PortabilityCan run on-prem / other cloudsMainly AWS; ECS Anywhere supports on-premises
When to chooseStandardized K8s, need portabilityWant simple AWS-native

run tasks on external instances; Kubernetes is still the choice when needing the Kubernetes ecosystem.

5. Fargate + EKS

Fargate can be used with EKS Fargate profile (select namespace/label → pod running on Fargate).

EKS is still the control plane layer; Workload must be compatible with Fargate capabilities (eg cannot run daemonset on Fargate).

6. Node groups, networking & persistent storage

1. EKS Node Management

  • Managed Node Group: AWS manages the EC2 Nodes and Auto Scaling Group (ASG) lifecycle.

  • Self-Managed Nodes: Self-manage EC2 Nodes, suitable when deep customization is needed.

  • Multi-AZ: Distribute Nodes in Private Subnets in multiple AZs to increase HA.

  • ALB/NLB: Allows application access from outside.

  • NAT Gateway / VPC Endpoints: Let Nodes/Pods access the Internet or AWS Services when needed.

2. EKS Storage

Kubernetes uses PVC (PersistentVolumeClaim) to request storage.

Pod → PVC → StorageClass → CSI Driver
                            |
                            v
                      AWS Storage
                      (EBS / EFS / FSx)

Storage

When to use?

EBS

Block Storage, attached to EC2 Node in the same AZ

EFS

Shared File System multi-AZ, multiple Pods can access at the same time

FSx

HPC, high performance or specialized filesystem

EKS Fargate Limitations

  • Does not support EBS Volume: Use EFS if you need Persistent Shared Storage.

  • Does not support DaemonSet: Does not run workloads that require direct management of the host.

  • No need to manage EC2 Nodes: AWS Fargate provides compute.

Quickly remember SAA-C03

  • Managed Node Group → AWS manages EC2 Nodes.

  • EKS EC2 + EBS → Persistent Block Storage.

  • EKS Fargate + EFS → Persistent Shared Storage.

  • HPC + EKS EC2 → FSx for Lustre.

  • Fargate → No EBS, no DaemonSet.

7. Kubernetes resources and permissions for Pods

DemandService / conceptFunction
Run Kubernetes on AWSAmazon EKSAWS manages the Kubernetes control plane
Compute runs PodsWorker Nodes (EC2/Fargate)EC2 nodes or serverless Fargate are where the workload actually runs
Maintain 3 Pod replicasDeploymentMaintain desired replicas, create replacement Pods when Pods fail
Stable networking to PodsKubernetes ServiceStable network address even if Pods are replaced or IP changed
Organize Kubernetes resourcesNamespaceGroup Pods, Services, Deployments by team/environment
Identity for Kubernetes PodsService AccountThe identity of the Pod when calling the Kubernetes API; RBAC combination
Non-sensitive configurationConfigMapSave URLs, environment variables, log levels outside the image
Sensitive application dataKubernetes Secret / AWS Secrets ManagerPasswords, API keys, tokens; Secrets Manager supports rotation when configured
Kubernetes API permissionsRBACRegulate who can view/create/edit/delete Kubernetes resources
AWS permissions per Pod + OIDCIRSAService Account ↔ IAM role via IAM OIDC provider + STS; grant temporary credentials
AWS permissions per Pod + EKS AgentEKS Pod IdentityService Account ↔ IAM role via Pod Identity Agent + EKS Auth; No need for IAM OIDC provider
IAM permissions for EC2 worker nodeEC2 Instance ProfileAttach the IAM role node to the EC2 worker node's AWS tasks

Demand

Select

AWS Service needs permission to manage resources for you

Service-Linked Role

EKS Pods need AWS permissions, using OIDC

IRSA

EKS Pods require AWS permissions, simple configuration, do not use OIDC Provider

EKS Pod Identity

EKS runs Pods on Fargate, requiring its own IAM Role

IRSA

ECS Application needs to access S3

ECS Task Role

RBAC authorization in Kubernetes; The IAM role grants permissions to AWS. Node roles do not replace individual Pod rights. Restrict Pod access to IMDS to avoid obtaining node credentials.

EKS Pod Identity Pods supported on Linux EC2 nodes, Fargate not supported; Fargate Pods can use IRSA. Base64 in a Kubernetes Secret is not encryption; Still need to control access and avoid putting secrets in image/Git. ·

8. Two flows grant the IAM role to the Pod

IRSA — OIDC + STS

Pod uses Kubernetes Service Account
↓ SA annotation: eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn
Kubernetes issues projected OIDC Token to Pod
↓ AWS SDK in Pod calls AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity
AWS STS
├── Authenticate OIDC Token via IAM OIDC provider
└── Check IAM Role Trust Policy (issuer, sub, audit)
↓
STS returns Temporary Credentials to Pod
↓
Pod calls S3/DynamoDB according to IAM Permission Policy

Configuration: IAM OIDC provider for cluster + IAM role with Trust/Permission Policy + Service Account annotation. Trust policy properly limits namespace/Service Account; Use the AWS SDK that supports IRSA.

EKS Pod Identity — Agent + EKS Auth

Pod A uses Service Account: training-sa, namespace: ml
↓
Kubernetes grants projected Pod Identity Token (JWT)
↓ AWS SDK sends token to Agent on node
Pod Identity Agent
↓ EKS Auth: AssumeRoleForPodIdentity
EKS Auth API
├── Token authentication
├── Check Pod Identity Association
└── Map cluster + ml + training-sa → S3ReadRole
↓ EKS calls STS AssumeRole; The role trust policy must allow it
Temporary Credentials
↓ EKS Auth → Agent → Pod
Pod calls S3 according to IAM Permission Policy

Configuration: Pod Identity Agent (or integrated EKS Auto Mode) + association between cluster/namespace/Service Account and IAM role. Role trust pods.eks.amazonaws.com with sts:AssumeRole, sts:TagSession; node role needed eks-auth:AssumeRoleForPodIdentity. Use an SDK that supports container credential providers.

CriteriaIRSAEKS Pod Identity
Role linkService Account annotationPod Identity Association
AuthenticationIAM OIDC provider + STSAgent + EKS Auth; No need for IAM OIDC provider
STSPod SDK calls AssumeRoleWithWebIdentityEKS calls AssumeRole
CredentialsSTS → PodEKS Auth → Agent → Pod

Trust policy decides Who gets to assume the role?; permission policy decides What can roles do?. ·

9. Use cases

Use cases:

The team already has Kubernetes manifests/Helm charts and needs to keep the Kubernetes API/tooling when moving to AWS.

Stateful pods on EC2 nodes using EBS; multiple pods sharing files or Fargate pods using EFS when appropriate.

10. Five EKS Compute modes, Autoscaling Streams & Three easily confused Endpoints

EKS Scaling/Compute componentExact role
Kubernetes Metrics ServerCollect actual CPU/RAM of Pods/Nodes assigned to HPA (do not create/delete Pods yourself)
HPA vs VPAHorizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) increase/decrease number of Pods; Vertical Pod Autoscaler (VPA) Adjust CPU/RAM requests of each Pod
Cluster Autoscaler vs KarpenterCluster Autoscaler increases/decreases nodes via ASG; Karpenter Direct allocation of EC2 optimizes Pending Pods configuration without being tied to a fixed ASG
EKS Auto ModeAWS automatically manages compute, autoscaling, networking, and core add-ons
Load increases → Metrics Server → HPA increases the number of Pods → Lack of space (Pending Pods) → Karpenter / Cluster Autoscaler adds EC2 Nodes
The three Endpoints around EKS are easy to confuseFunction
EKS Interface VPC EndpointCall the AWS EKS Management API (eg DescribeCluster) qua PrivateLink
Kubernetes Private API Endpoint / Kubernetes Public API EndpointWorker Nodes & kubectl communicate with Control Plane (turn on Private Endpoint for pure internal connection; if only enable Kubernetes Public API Endpoint, the private node needs NAT Gateway to join)
ECR Interface Endpoints + S3 Gateway EndpointWorker Nodes in private subnets without the Internet can still pull container images and image layers from S3
Quick review · SAA-C03

EKS manages the Kubernetes control plane; Workloads can run on node groups or Fargate. IAM and Kubernetes RBAC are two separate permission layers.

Serverless Overview

AWS Lambda

Purpose: AWS Lambda is used to run code based on events without needing to manage the server.

Event
↓
Lambda
↓
Run Code
↓
Result / Action

AWS takes care of itself: Server · Scaling · Runtime infrastructure

You mainly worry about: Code · Memory · Timeout · Permissions · Concurrency

Lambda = serverless compute for short-term, event-driven workloads.

1. Important limits

Memory: 128 MB → 10 GB

Increasing memory also increases: CPU · Network

Timeout: Max execution = 15 minutes

Temporary storage: /tmp: 512 MB → 10 GB = temporary / ephemeral

Do not use /tmp to save data long term.

Environment variables: Total size · ≈ 4 KB

2. Lambda Package

There are 2 popular ways: ZIP · or · Container Image

2.1. ZIP

Suitable for small application/dependency.

Code + Dependencies
↓
ZIP
↓
Lambda

2.2. Container Image

Container Image
↓
Lambda Runtime API compatible
↓
Lambda

Lambda containers are not places to run arbitrary Docker. If you need a long-running container or more runtime control → ECS/Fargate is often more suitable.

3. Event-driven

Lambdas are often triggered by: API Gateway · S3 · SQS · EventBridge · DynamoDB Streams · SNS

For example:

S3 Upload
↓
Lambda
↓
Create Thumbnail

Or:

API Gateway
↓
Lambda
↓
Business Logic
↓
Response

4. Pricing Model

Lambda mainly charges according to: Number of Requests + Compute Duration

Compute dependencies: Memory · × · Execution Time

Points to remember:

Higher memory
→ More powerful CPU
→ function can run faster

Should have the lowest memory not always the cheapest.

5. Concurrency

Concurrency = number of Lambda invocations running at the same time.

50 requests/second

Each request runs 2 seconds
↓
≈ 100 concurrent executions

Not: Concurrency ≠ total number of requests

6. Reserved Concurrency

Reserved concurrency has just been used to: Keep capacity for function + Maximum concurrency limit

Lambda A · Reserved Concurrency = 100

→ Lambda A has a maximum of about 100 invocations running at the same time.

This helps protect downstream:

Lambda
↓
RDS

If Lambda scaling is too strong:

1000 Lambdas
↓
1000 DB Connections
↓
RDS overload

Possible limits: Reserved Concurrency = 100

7. Throttling

When concurrency runs out:

Request
↓
Lambda
↓
No available concurrency
↓
Throttle

With synchronous invocation:

Throttle
→ error / HTTP 429

With async invocation:

Throttle / Failure
↓
Retry
↓
DLQ / Destination if configured

Important metrics: Throttles

8. Cold Start

Cold Start = Lambda must create a new execution environment before running the code.

Request
↓
There is no warm environment
↓
Load Code
↓
Start Runtime
↓
Init Code
↓
Handler

→ The first request may be slower.

When the environment is still warm:

Request
↓
Existing Environment
↓
Handler

→ faster.

9. Reduce Cold Start

There are 2 main concepts:

9.1. Provisioned Concurrency

AWS keeps the initialized Lambda environment available.

Provisioned Environment
↓
Request
↓
Run now

Suitable: API needs stable latency · Predictable traffic

9.2. SnapStart

Idea:

Initialize Function
↓
Snapshot State
↓
New request
↓
Restore Snapshots

→ reduces initialization time for supported runtimes.

Remember: Provisioned Concurrency = keep the environment warm

SnapStart = restore from inited snapshot

10. CloudWatch Monitoring

Lambda integrates itself with CloudWatch.

Follow: Invocations · Duration · Errors · Throttles · Logs

Lambda
↓
CloudWatch Metrics + Logs
↓
Dashboard / Alarm
Errors > 5
↓
CloudWatch Alarm
↓
SNS

11. Default Lambda and VPC

By default, Lambda runs in a network managed by AWS.

Lambda
↓
Internet / AWS Public APIs

But you cannot access private resources in your VPC:

Private RDS · Private ElastiCache · Internal Load Balancer

12. Lambda into VPC

If you need to access private RDS:

Lambda
↓
Attach VPC
↓
Subnets + Security Group
↓
ENI
↓
RDS

ENI = virtual network interface that Lambda uses to connect to the VPC.

Lambda
↓
ENI + Lambda Security Group
↓
Private Subnet
↓
RDS Security Group
↓
RDS

RDS SG should allow: Source = Lambda Security Group

instead of opening the CIDR wide.

13. Lambda in VPC wants to go to the Internet

Khi Lambda attach VPC:

Lambda
↓
Private Subnet

it does not have its own public IP.

If Internet is needed:

Lambda
↓
Private Subnet
↓
NAT Gateway
↓
Internet Gateway
↓
Internet

If you only need AWS services:

Lambda
↓
VPC Endpoint
↓
AWS Service

→ keep traffic private when the service supports it.

14. Lambda + RDS

Common problem:

Traffic increased
↓
Strong lambda scale
↓
multiple DB Connections
↓
RDS overload

Solution:

Lambda
↓
RDS Proxy
↓
RDS / Aurora

15. RDS Proxy

RDS Proxy = connection pool between Lambda and RDS/Aurora.

Lambda 1 ─┐
Lambda 2 ─┼→ RDS Proxy → RDS
Lambda 3 ─┘

Instead:

Lambda 1 → DB Connection
Lambda 2 → DB Connection
Lambda 3 → DB Connection
...

Proxies help: Reuse connections · Reduce connection storm · Support failover · Better credential management

16. Flow Lambda + Private RDS

Lambda
↓
VPC
↓
ENI
↓
RDS Proxy
↓
RDS / Aurora

If Lambda still needs Internet:

Lambda
↓
Private Subnet
↓
NAT Gateway
↓
Internet

17. Lambda vs ECS/Fargate

Lambda = event-driven = short workload = max 15 minutes = self-scaling invocation

ECS / Fargate = container workload = long runs = more runtime control

Resize image
→ Lambda
Backend containers run 24/7
→ ECS / Fargate
