19. Lambda Layers, SnapStart, Runtime, Memory–CPU Ratio & Hyperplane ENI

Lambda featureThe core point needs to be distinguished
Lambda LayersReuse common libraries/code between multiple functions; each Layer version is immutable — publishing a new version does not automatically change the function pointing to the old version (large data hundreds of MB–GB read/write at runtime, use EFS)
Lambda SnapStartTake a snapshot of the initialized environment to drastically reduce cold starts; only works on published versions (not applicable to $LATEST)
Runtime & Container ImageSupports any language via Custom Runtime and Linux container images up to 10 GB (Windows containers not supported)
Memory ↔ CPU ratioIncreasing RAM will automatically increase CPU scaling and network bandwidth (~1.769 MB equivalent to 1 vCPU; max 10.240 MB ≈ 6 vCPUs; separate from /tmp and 4 KB environment variable limit)
VPC Configuration (Hyperplane ENI)Only turn on when calling private IP (RDS, ElastiCache, Internal EC2, Cross-VPC RDS Proxy); Does not need to be enabled when calling public AWS APIs such as S3/SQS/DynamoDB. Lambda and EC2 and VPC are not required to be in the same subnet
Quick review · SAA-C03

Lambda is event-driven compute, limited to 15 minutes runtime; concurrency, timeout, memory, /tmp and IAM Execution Role are important points.

Amazon DynamoDB

Purpose: Amazon DynamoDB is a serverless NoSQL database with low latency, self-scaling and no need for server management.

DynamoDB = NoSQL = Serverless = Key-value / Document = Millisecond latency

Suitable for: User Profile · Shopping Cart · Session · Game Data · IoT Metadata · Serverless API

DynamoDB is strong when it knows access patterns in advance and accesses by key.

1. Table / Item / Attribute

Table = data table

Item = a record

Attribute = field of record

For example:

Users Table

Item:
{
  UserId: 123,
  Name: "Trong",
  Age: 25
}

Besides the Primary Key, the schema is quite flexible:

Item A
→ Name + Age

Item B
→ Name + Country + Email

2. Primary Key

Primary Key must be selected when creating the table.

2.1. Simple Primary Key

Partition Key

UserId = 123

Each Partition Key must be unique.

2.2. Composite Primary Key

Partition Key + Sort Key

UserId + GameId

Data: User1 + Game1 · User1 + Game2 · User1 + Game3

→ the same user has many items.

Remember: Partition Key = data group

Sort Key = differentiate items in the same group

3. Item Size

Max Item Size = 400 KB

Item may contain: String · Number · Boolean · Binary · Null · List · Map · String / Number / Binary Set

4. Capacity Mode

There are 2 main types.

4.1. Provisioned

You choose first: · RCU · WCU

RCU = Read Capacity

WCU = Write Capacity

Suitable: Traffic is predictable

Can use Auto Scaling.

4.2. On-Demand

Traffic increased
↓
DynamoDB scales itself

Traffic decreased
↓
scale down

You pay according to usage request.

Traffic is difficult to predict · Sudden spike

5. DAX

DAX = in-memory cache specific to DynamoDB.

Application
↓
DAX
↓
DynamoDB

If cache hits:

Application
↓
DAX
↓
Results are very fast

If cache miss:

DAX
↓
DynamoDB
↓
get data
↓
cache again

DAX can reduce read latency to microseconds.

6. DAX vs ElastiCache

DAX = DynamoDB cache = DynamoDB API compatible

ElastiCache = general cache = Redis / Valkey / Memcached

DynamoDB reads too much
→ DAX

Cache sessions / aggregates / custom objects
→ ElastiCache

7. DynamoDB Streams

Streams = records item changes.

INSERT
MODIFY
DELETE
↓
DynamoDB Streams

Popular flows:

DynamoDB
↓
Streams
↓
Lambda
↓
Action
User Created
↓
DynamoDB Streams
↓
Lambda
↓
Send Welcome Email

Streams keep change records between:

24 hours

Streams = change feed, not backup.

8. DynamoDB + Kinesis Data Streams

If you need a stronger stream:

DynamoDB
↓
Kinesis Data Streams
↓
Firehose / Lambda / Analytics
↓
S3 / Redshift / OpenSearch

Suitable when needed: More consumers · Longer retention · Analytics pipeline

9. Global Tables

Global Tables = DynamoDB multi-Region active-active.

US Region · ↕ · EU Region · ↕ · Asia Region

Applications can: Read + Write · in Region near the user

Data is replicated between Regions.

Global Application · Low Latency · Multi-Region DR

Streams = broadcast change events to consumers. Global Tables = replicate table between Regions.

10. TTL

TTL = automatically delete item when expired.

Item has attributes: expiresAt = epoch timestamp

Session
↓
TTL expired
↓
DynamoDB deletes itself

Session · Token · Temporary Data · Old Events

TTL runs in the background, not guaranteed to be deleted exactly at the second of expiration.

11. Backup

11.1. Point-in-Time Recovery (PITR)

Continuous Backup
↓
Restore to a previous time

According to the slides: Recovery Window = 35 days

10:00 data is correct
11:00 User mistakenly deleted
↓
Restore to 10:00

Restore creation: New DynamoDB Table

11.2. On-Demand Backup

Create Backup
↓
keep until you delete

Long-term Backup · Audit · Compliance · DR

12. Export to S3

DynamoDB
↓
Export
↓
S3

Athena Analytics · Audit · ETL · Archive

There is no need to consume the read capacity of the table.

13. Import from S3

S3
↓
Import
↓
New DynamoDB Table

You can import formats like: CSV · DynamoDB JSON · ION

Import always creates new table.

14. DynamoDB vs RDS

DynamoDB = NoSQL = key-based access = large scale = flexible schema

RDS / Aurora = SQL = relational = joins = complex transactions/business data

Shopping Cart / Session
→ DynamoDB
Order + Customer + Payment
need join/relationship
→ RDS / Aurora

15. Popular flow

API Gateway
↓
Lambda
↓
DynamoDB

Can add:

DynamoDB
↓
Streams
↓
Lambda
↓
SNS / OpenSearch / S3

Or:

Application
↓
DAX
↓
DynamoDB

16. Use Cases

Use cases:

Serverless API
↓
Lambda
↓
DynamoDB
Shopping Cart
↓
DynamoDB
↓
TTL automatically deletes old data
DynamoDB
↓
Streams
↓
Lambda
↓
Notification

↓
US / EU / Asia

17. DynamoDB Deletion Protection vs TTL

DynamoDB FeaturesPurpose & Mechanism of action
Deletion ProtectionPrevent accidental deletion of entire DynamoDB tables at the admin level
TTL (Time to Live)Automatically asynchronously purge overdue items based on the epoch timestamp attribute (free WCU at original table, may charge for replication at Global Tables)
Quick review · SAA-C03

DynamoDB is serverless NoSQL key-value/document; The partition/sort key design affects the query. DAX is used to cache DynamoDB.

Amazon API Gateway

Purpose: Amazon API Gateway is a managed service that exposes APIs to clients and sits between the client and the backend.

Client
↓
API Gateway
↓
Lambda / HTTP Backend / AWS Service

API Gateway takes care of common parts such as: Authentication · Authorization · Throttling · Validation · Caching · Routing · Stages / Versions

API Gateway = API gateway.

1. API Types

API Gateway supports: REST API · HTTP API · WebSocket API

For example:

Mobile App
↓
REST / HTTP API
↓
Lambda

WebSocket is used when real-time two-way connection is needed:

Client · ↕ · WebSocket API · ↕ · Backend

2. Stage & Version

Environment can be divided: dev · test · prod

/dev/orders · /test/orders · /prod/orders

Version API: v1 · v2

→ Helps deploy new version without destroying old client.

3. Throttling / API Key

API Gateway can limit requests:

Client
↓
API Gateway
↓
Rate Limit
↓
Backend

100 requests / second

If exceeded: Throttle

Can be used: API Key + Usage Plan

to limit by client.

4. Validation & Transform

API Gateway can validate requests before sending them to the backend.

POST /users

body:
{
  "email": ...
}

If fields are missing:

API Gateway
↓
Reject

no need to call Lambda.

You can also transform: Request · Response

before/after backend.

5. API Caching

API Gateway can cache responses.

Client
↓
API Gateway Cache
↓
Response

If cache hits: no need to call backend

→ reduce: Latency · Backend Load · Cost

Suitable for GET/read data with little change.

Do not use cache to cover writes or constantly changing data.

6. Integration Types

6.1. Lambda Integration

Client
↓
API Gateway
↓
Lambda
↓
Business Logic

This is a popular serverless pattern.

6.2. HTTP Integration

API Gateway can call existing HTTP backends:

Client
↓
API Gateway
↓
ALB / HTTP API / On-prem

Use when you want to add: Auth · Rate Limit · API Key · Caching

before the current backend.

6.3. AWS Service Integration

API Gateway can directly call AWS service.

Client
↓
API Gateway
↓
SQS

Or:

Client
↓
API Gateway
↓
Step Functions

→ sometimes there is no need for an intermediary Lambda.

6.4. VPC Link and Cloud Map

VPC Link is the connection for API Gateway to call the private backend in VPC; The backend does not need a public IP.

Client
↓
API Gateway
↓ VPC Link
Internal ALB / NLB
↓
Private backend

With HTTP API, can be integrated via AWS Cloud Map: API Gateway uses service discovery to find the IP and port of the registered backend, Load Balancer is not required.

HTTP API Gateway
↓ VPC Link + Cloud Map discovery
IP:port of healthy service instances
↓
Private backend / ECS service

Private integration describes the API → backend path; Private API endpoint Describes the client → API path. APIs with private backends can still be called by clients via a public endpoint.

7. Event Ingestion Example

Client
↓
API Gateway
↓
Kinesis Data Streams
↓
Firehose
↓
S3

Suitable for: Logs · Telemetry · Clickstream · Events

8. Endpoint Types

8.1. Edge-Optimized

Global Client
↓
CloudFront Edge
↓
API Gateway

Suitable for global clients.

8.2. Regional

Client
↓
Regional API Gateway

Suitable when the client/backend is concentrated in a Region.

You can manually add CloudFront in front if you want.

8.3. Private

VPC
↓
Interface VPC Endpoint
↓
Private API Gateway

Only access from private network/VPC is allowed.

9. Security

  • Common auth types: IAM = internal AWS application
  • Cognito User Pool = mobile / web users
  • Custom Authorizer = custom auth logic

Flow example:

User Login
↓
Cognito
↓
JWT Token
↓
API Gateway
↓
Lambda

Resource Policy for REST API

Resource Policy attaches directly to the REST API to control who gets to call the API (execute-api:Invoke), for example by AWS account/principal, source IP, VPC or VPC endpoint.

AWS Console
↓
API Gateway → REST APIs → Select API → Resource policy

Policy coordinates with the method's authorization mechanism; Explicit Deny still beats Allow. After editing the policy, redeploy the REST API for the changes to take effect.

10. Custom Domain

You can use your own domain:

api.example.com
↓
API Gateway

Need: ACM Certificate + Route 53

Endpoint edge-optimized:

Certificate
→ us-east-1

Regional endpoint:

Certificate
→ same Region as API

11. OpenAPI / Swagger

API definitions can be imported:

OpenAPI / Swagger
↓
API Gateway

→ quickly create routes, methods and models.

12. Use Cases

Use cases:

Mobile App
↓
Cognito
↓
API Gateway
↓
Lambda

↓
API Gateway

Internal ALB
Telemetry

13. Usage Plan, Canary Release, ACM Region & Cloud Map

Mechanism on API GatewayDesign rules
Usage Plan + API KeyLimit Rate, Burst and Quota according to each client on REST API (exceeds payment threshold HTTP 429); API Key specifies the package identifier, Do not use in place of authentication/authorization IAM or Cognito
Canary Release (REST API Stage)Split a small percentage of traffic (eg 10%) to the new version for testing before promoting to 100%
ACM Certificate by EndpointRegional / HTTP API Use ACM certification same Region; Edge-Optimized REST API Using CloudFront managed by AWS, ACM certification is required us-east-1
Private IntegrationVPC Link backend connection in VPC; private HTTP API Supports direct integration with AWS Cloud Map to discover the service IP/port without forcing a Load Balancer
Quick review · SAA-C03

API Gateway is a managed front door for APIs; Need to distinguish REST/HTTP/WebSocket API, throttling, authorization and backend integration.

AWS Step Functions

Purpose: Coordinate multiple processing steps with clear status, retry, and error handling.

1. State machine

Workflow includes states such as Task, Choice, Parallel, Map, Wait, Succeed and Fail. Tasks can call Lambda or AWS service integrations, not just connect Lambda to Lambda.

Receive order → validate → inventory/payment → Choice
                                        ├─ success → delivery
                                        └─ error → compensation processing / failure reporting

2. Retry, Catch and side effects

Retry handles temporary errors of a step; Catch switches to the error handling branch. Set timeout to avoid waiting indefinitely.

The retried task can perform the action again, so idempotency should be designed for that
pay/send order.
Workflow does not automatically rollback all changes to external services; Use steps
compensation when needed.

3. Standard and Express

TypeSuitable
StandardWorkflow is long, needs history/audit; Maximum one year, job/callback support according to integration
ExpressHigh event rate, short tasks; Maximum 5 minutes, mainly request-response

Standard uses exactly-once workflow execution according to the service model, but Retry can still call the task again.

Asynchronous Express
= at-least-once; Synchronous Express is at-most-once.

Don't understand execution guarantees as side effect guarantees that never overlap. AWS workflow types.

4. Human approval

Standard workflow can wait for callback/task token for approval or external job.

The system sends success/failure results in tokens with appropriate permissions; set timeout and branch when there is no response.

The state machine role needs permission to call services; The task's network/permission must still be correct.

5. Use cases

Use cases: Order fulfillment with multiple steps, data processing with parallel/Map, workflow requires human approval. A simple short task that doesn't require additional Step Functions; A multi-state/retry sequence is appropriate.

Quick review · SAA-C03

Step Functions coordinates multi-step workflows, handling state/retry/error; Lambda executes code, not replacing the entire orchestration workflow.

Amazon Cognito

Purpose: Amazon Cognito is used to manage identity for users of web/mobile apps.

User Pool
= login user
= pay JWT tokens

Identity Pool
= change identity/token
→ temporary AWS credentials

User Pool = Authentication Identity Pool = AWS Access

1. Cognito User Pool

User Pool is user directory for application.

Support: Sign up / Sign in · Username / Email / Password · Password Reset · Email / Phone Verification · MFA · Social Login · SAML

User
↓
Cognito User Pool
↓
Login successful
↓
JWT Token

The token can then be used to call the API:

Client
↓ JWT
API Gateway
↓
Validate Token
↓
Lambda / Backend

2. When is User Pool used?

For example, a web app needs: Register · Login · Forgot Password · MFA · Google Login

→ use: Cognito User Pool

User Pool = "Who are you?"

3. Cognito Identity Pool

Identity Pool is used to change the authenticated identity to:

Temporary AWS Credentials

User Login
↓
User Pool / Google / Facebook / SAML
↓
Token
↓
Identity Pool
↓
Temporary AWS Credentials
↓
S3 / DynamoDB / AWS Service

Credentials are limited by: IAM Role + IAM Policy

4. Why do we need Identity Pool?

For example, mobile app needs to upload directly to private S3.

Shouldn't:

Mobile App
↓
Hard-code AWS Access Key ❌

Should:

User Login
↓
Identity Pool
↓
Temporary Credentials
↓
Private S3

No need to embed permanent AWS access key into the app.

5. Authenticated vs Guest

Identity Pool can grant different roles:

Authenticated User
→ IAM Role A
Guest / Unauthenticated User
→ IAM Role B

For example:

Logged-in User
→ Read + Write S3

Guest
→ Read only

6. User Pool vs Identity Pool

User Pool = login user = JWT token

Identity Pool = temporary AWS credential = direct access AWS services

User
↓
User Pool
↓
JWT
↓
API Gateway

Other than:

User
↓
User Pool
↓ JWT
Identity Pool
↓
Temporary IAM Credentials
↓
S3 / DynamoDB

7. DynamoDB Fine-Grained Access

Identity Pool can combine IAM so that each user only reads their own data.

User A
→ only read UserA data

UserB
→ only read UserB data

Can be used: dynamodb:LeadingKeys

with identity ID to limit the partition key.

User A
↓
Temporary Credentials
↓
DynamoDB
↓
only item has User A's Partition Key

8. Cognito does not replace IAM

Cognito = end users of application

IAM = AWS users / roles / services / administrators

Customer logs into the mobile app
→ Cognito
Developer manages AWS account
→ IAM

9. Use Cases

Use cases:

Mobile App
↓
Cognito User Pool
↓
Login + MFA
↓
API Gateway
↓
Lambda
Mobile App
↓
Identity Pool
↓
Temporary Credentials
↓
S3 Upload
Multi-user App
↓
Identity Pool
↓
DynamoDB
↓
Each user only reads his or her own data

10. Cognito Adaptive Authentication (Risk-Based)

User login → Cognito Risk-Based Adaptive Authentication scores risk (IP, geolocation, device, history)
  ├─ Low Risk → Allows normal login
  └─ High Risk → Automatically force MFA authentication or block access
Quick review · SAA-C03

Cognito User Pool authenticates users; Identity Pool grants temporary AWS credentials to access AWS resources according to role.

CloudFront Functions & Lambda@Edge

Purpose: Both are used to run logic right near the user at the CloudFront edge location before/after the request goes to the origin.

CloudFront Functions = logic is very light, very fast

Lambda@Edge = stronger, more flexible logic

CloudFront Functions = lightweight Lambda@Edge = powerful edge logic

1. Flow CloudFront

Viewer
↓
Viewer Request
↓
CloudFront Cache
↓
Origin Request
↓
Origin
↓
Origin Response
↓
Viewer Response
↓
Viewer

CloudFront Functions only runs in: Viewer Request · Viewer Response

Lambda@Edge can be run in: Viewer Request · Origin Request · Origin Response · Viewer Response

2. CloudFront Functions

CloudFront Functions used JavaScript for extremely lightweight logic.

Suitable: Rewrite URL · Redirect · Modify Headers · Modify Cookies · Normalize Query String · Lightweight Auth Check

For example:

/old-page
↓
CloudFront Function
↓
Redirect
↓
/new-page

Or:

?utm_source=abc
↓
Normalize
↓
Cache Key

→ helps increase cache hits.

3. When to use CloudFront Functions?

Use cases:

Use when logical: Very fast · No need for network calls · No need for filesystem · No need for request body · No need for large dependencies

Suitable for very large request volume and simple logic.

4. Lambda@Edge

Lambda@Edge uses Lambda code deployed to CloudFront edge.

CloudFront
↓
Lambda@Edge
↓
Complex Edge Logic

Can be used: Node.js · Python

Code is generated at:

us-east-1
↓
CloudFront replicate
↓
Edge Locations

5. Where is Lambda@Edge stronger?

Can do things CloudFront Functions can't do:

Network access · Request body · Filesystem · Dependencies · More complex logic

Viewer Request
↓
Lambda@Edge
↓
Call external service
↓
Validate token
↓
Continue / Reject
Request
↓
Lambda@Edge
↓
Choose Origin
↓
US Origin / EU Origin

6. Viewer vs Origin Events

6.1. Viewer Request

User sends request
↓
CloudFront receives
↓
Viewer Request Function

Used for: Rewrite URL · Redirect · Check Header · Normalize Cache Key

6.2. Origin Request

Lambda@Edge only.

Cache Miss
↓
CloudFront
↓
Origin Request
↓
Origin

Change Origin · Add Auth Header · Complex Request Logic

6.3. Origin Response

Origin
↓
Response
↓
Lambda@Edge
↓
CloudFront

Used to adjust: Error Response · Headers · Response Logic

6.4. Viewer Response

CloudFront
↓
Viewer Response
↓
User

Add Security Headers · Modify Cookies · Modify Response Headers

7. CloudFront Functions vs Lambda@Edge

CloudFront Functions = JavaScript = very fast = lightweight logic = Viewer Request / Response

Lambda@Edge = Node.js / Python = more powerful = has network / body / filesystem = Viewer + Origin events

8. For example, select service

8.1. Redirect URL

/old
→ /new

→ use: CloudFront Functions

8.2. Add security headers

Content-Security-Policy · X-Frame-Options

→ commonly used:

8.3. Choose origin according to request

US User
→ US Origin

EU User
→ EU Origin

Lambda@Edge

8.4. Call external API to verify

Request
↓
Edge Function
↓
External API

→ need: CloudFront Functions does not have network access.

9. Do not change the Backend

Edge functions should not be used for: Database Transaction · Long-running Business Logic · Heavy Processing · Stateful Workflow · Container Workload

Those things should still be: Lambda · ECS · Backend Application

10. Use Cases

Quick review · SAA-C03

CloudFront Functions runs very light logic at the edge; Lambda@Edge is more flexible but has different limitations/implementation.

AWS AppSync

Purpose: Build a completely serverless GraphQL API and real-time update channel (Pub/Sub WebSockets), combining multiple data sources in a single query.

1. Core characteristics

AWS AppSync: managed GraphQL & Pub/Sub API services allow clients to retrieve only the necessary data fields from DynamoDB, Aurora/RDS, OpenSearch, Lambda or HTTP APIs through a single endpoint.

2. Compare AWS AppSync vs Amazon API Gateway

CriteriaAWS AppSyncAmazon API Gateway
Primary communication modelGraphQL & Real-time Pub/Sub Subscriptions (Pre-managed WebSockets)REST API, HTTP API and the general WebSocket API
Merge multi-source dataResolvers collect data from multiple tables/services in one request, avoiding over-fetchingOften a composite Lambda/backend layer is needed or the client has to call multiple REST endpoints
Authentication supportAPI Key, IAM, Cognito User Pools, OIDC and Lambda AuthorizerIAM, Cognito User Pools, Lambda Authorizer, Mutual TLS

Data & Analytics

Amazon Athena

Purpose: Amazon Athena is a serverless SQL query service to analyze data primarily located in Amazon S3 without needing to build a database/data warehouse server.

Athena = SQL + S3 + Serverless

The file is in S3
↓
Athena
↓
SELECT ... FROM ...
↓
Results

Athena = runs SQL directly on data in S3.

1. Basic Flow

Producer
↓
S3
↓
Glue Data Catalog
↓
Athena
↓
SQL Query
↓
Query Result
↓
S3
↓
QuickSight / Application

In which: S3 = contains real data

Glue Data Catalog = table description / schema / partition

Athena = run SQL

2. What can Athena read?

Athena can query files such as: CSV · JSON · Parquet · ORC · Avro

For example:

s3://logs/orders/
↓
Athena
↓
SELECT *
FROM orders
WHERE year = 2026;

No need to import data into Athena first.

3. Serverless

Athena does not need: Provision server · Choose instance · Manage cluster · Patch server

You just:

There is data in S3
↓
Definition of table/schema
↓
Run SQL

Without a query, there is almost no compute Athena has to run for that query.

4. Pricing: Data Scanned

Athena mainly charges according to: The amount of data scanned by the query

According to the slides: ≈ $5 / TB scanned

So:

Scan less
↓
Query is cheaper
+
usually faster

Prices may vary by Region/time.

5. Data Scanned example

Let's say S3 has: 1 TB CSV data

Query: SELECT name · FROM users;

With CSV, Athena may have to read a lot of file data.

If the same data is saved using Parquet:

Athena just needs to read the column "name"

→ scan less.

6. Parquet / ORC

Parquet and ORC are columnar formats.

For example the table has: Name · Age · Country · Salary · Email

SELECT Name, Salary · FROM employees;

CSV: often have to read the entire row/file

Parquet: read only: · Name · Salary

→ reduce scanned data.

Parquet / ORC = less scans = faster = cheaper

7. Compression

Files can be compressed: gzip · Snappy · zstd · ...

Data
↓
Compression
↓
Smaller files
↓
Athena reads fewer bytes

→ can reduce cost and reading time.

8. Partition

Partition = divide data in S3 into groups according to folder/prefix.

s3://logs/ · year=2026/ · month=09/ · day=29/

Athena understands: year · month · day

as partition columns.

SELECT * · FROM logs · WHERE year = 2026 · AND month = 9;

Athena simply read: year=2026/month=09/

instead of the entire bucket.

9. Partition is Virtual Column

For example, the Parquet file inside does not necessarily contain:

But S3 path: /year=2026/month=09/day=29/

Tell Athena: year = 2026 · month = 09 · day = 29

Partition value can come from the S3 path, not necessarily in the file.

10. If not filter Partition

Suppose: 2024/ · 2025/ · 2026/

SELECT * · FROM logs;

→ Athena can scan everything.

If: WHERE year = 2026

→ only scan partition 2026.

11. Glue + Athena

Raw data:

CSV / JSON
↓
AWS Glue
↓
Convert
↓
Parquet + Partition
↓
S3
↓
Athena

This is a very common pattern: Glue = prepare data

Athena = query data

12. Glue Data Catalog

Athena needs to know: What is the name of the table? · What columns? · What type of data? · Where is S3 location? · What partition?

Glue Data Catalog stores that metadata.

S3 = Data

Glue Data Catalog = Metadata

Athena = SQL Engine

13. Athena + QuickSight

Can create BI flow:

S3
↓
Athena
↓
QuickSight
↓
Dashboard
Sales Data on S3
↓
Athena SQL
↓
QuickSight
↓
Sales Dashboard

14. Log Analytics

Athena is great for query logs stored in S3:

CloudTrail Logs · VPC Flow Logs · ALB / ELB Logs · Application Logs

CloudTrail
↓
S3
↓
Athena
↓
"Find all DeleteBucket API calls"

No need to build a separate log database.

15. Athena vs Redshift

Athena = direct query S3 = serverless = ad-hoc analytics = pay per data scanned

Redshift = Data Warehouse = data optimized for analytics = query/join/aggregate frequently

Occasionally query logs in S3
→ Athena
BI dashboard runs continuously
+ multiple joins / aggregates
→ Redshift

16. Federated Query

Athena doesn't just query S3.

Can query other sources through Data Source Connector.

Athena
↓
Lambda Connector
↓
DynamoDB / RDS / DocumentDB / ...
Athena SQL
↓
Lambda Connector
↓
DynamoDB
↓
Result

Sources may include: DynamoDB · Aurora / RDS · DocumentDB · Redshift · ElastiCache · On-prem Database · ...

17. Federated Query Flow

Analyst
↓
Athena SQL
↓
Lambda Data Source Connector
↓
External Database
↓
Return Data
↓
Athena
↓
Result saved to S3

Suitable when needed: Ad-hoc query · Join temporary data · Query multiple sources

This does not mean that Athena turns those databases into data warehouses.

18. File Size

Should avoid: 10 million files is very small

because Athena has to open and process too many objects.

Usually better: Fewer files + reasonably large files

Suggested slide ranges: > 128 MB / file

But this is a guideline, not a mandatory rule.

19. Athena optimization

Remember 4 main things: 1. Parquet / ORC · 2. Compression · 3. Partition · 4. Avoid too many small files

Ideal flow:

Raw CSV / JSON
↓
Glue
↓
Parquet
+ Compression
+ Partition
↓
S3
↓
Athena

20. Use Cases

Use cases:

CloudTrail Logs
↓
S3
↓
Athena
↓
Security Investigation
Raw Data
↓
Glue
↓
Parquet + Partition
↓
S3
↓
Athena
↓
QuickSight
S3 + DynamoDB / RDS
↓
Athena Federated Query
↓
Ad-hoc Analytics
Quick review · SAA-C03

Athena = Direct SQL on S3 data; Glue Data Catalog defines schema/partition; Parquet and partitioning help optimize the amount of scanned data.

Amazon Redshift

Purpose: Amazon Redshift is a data warehouse that uses SQL for analytics/BI, suitable for large queries, joins, aggregates and reporting on large amounts of data.

Redshift = Data Warehouse = SQL = OLAP

Not suitable as the main database for application transactions.

Order / Payment / Account Update
→ RDS / Aurora / DynamoDB

Analytics / Reporting / BI
→ Redshift

1. OLAP vs OLTP

1.1. OLTP

Used for application transactions: Create Order · Update User · Payment · Insert / Update small and frequently

Suitable: RDS · Aurora · DynamoDB

1.2. OLAP

Used to analyze a variety of data: SUM · COUNT · GROUP BY · JOIN · Reporting · Analytics

Redshift

Redshift = OLAP, not OLTP database.

2. Popular flow

RDS / Aurora / DynamoDB / S3
↓
ETL / ELT
↓
Redshift
↓
SQL Analytics
↓
QuickSight / Tableau

For example:

Sales Data
Product Data
Customer Data
↓
Redshift
↓
JOIN + GROUP BY
↓
Sales Dashboard

3. Why is Redshift fast for Analytics?

Redshift is optimized with: Columnar Storage · MPP · Sort Key · Distribution

4. Columnar Storage

Data is stored in columns instead of rows.

Table example: Name · Age · Country · Salary

Query: SELECT Country, AVG(Salary) · FROM employees · GROUP BY Country;

Redshift mainly needs to read: Country · Salary

instead of the entire row.

Columnar = scan fewer columns = suitable for analytics

5. MPP

MPP = Massively Parallel Processing

Redshift divides the query into multiple compute nodes for parallel processing.

SQL Query
↓
divided into several parts
↓
Compute Node 1
Compute Node 2
Compute Node 3
↓
parallel processing
↓
Result

MPP = multiple machines processing one large query.

6. Sort Key

Sort Key helps data to be sorted by columns commonly used for filtering.

Sort Key = OrderDate

WHERE OrderDate >= '2026-09-01'

→ Redshift can skip many unrelated data blocks.

Sort Key = reduce the data to be scanned

7. Distribution

Distribution decides how data is divided into compute nodes.

Data
↓
Compute Node 1
Compute Node 2
Compute Node 3

Objective: distribute workload evenly + Reduce data that must be moved when JOIN

Sort Key = how data is sorted. Distribution = which node the data is located at.

8. Cluster Architecture

In provisioned Redshift:

Client
↓
Leader Node
↓
Compute Nodes

8.1. Leader Node

Leader Node = receive SQL = create query plan = divide work among compute nodes = synthesize results

8.2. Compute Nodes

Compute Nodes = scan data = join = aggregate = parallel processing

QuickSight/Client
↓
Leader Node
↓
split query
↓
Compute Nodes
↓
parallel processing
↓
General leader
↓
Result

9. Provisioned vs Serverless

9.1. Provisioned

You choose: Node Type · Number of Nodes · Capacity

Redshift Cluster = always provisioned

Stable workload · Predictable traffic · Want to control capacity

But need: Capacity Planning · Resize · Monitor Cluster

9.2. Redshift Serverless

Do not manage clusters/nodes directly
↓
AWS provides compute on its own
↓
run query

Fluctuating traffic · Don't want to manage cluster · Ad-hoc / intermittent workload

Remember: Provisioned = you manage capacity

Serverless = AWS manages compute capacity

10. Redshift vs Athena

Both use SQL for analytics but work differently.

10.1. Athena

S3 Files
↓
Athena
↓
SQL

= query S3 directly = serverless = ad-hoc = pay per data scanned

10.2. Redshift

Data
↓
Load / Model into Warehouse
↓
Redshift
↓
SQL

= data warehouse = optimal for repeated queries = strong with large JOIN / Aggregates

Occasionally query CloudTrail logs
→ Athena
Dashboard sales runs continuously
+ multiple JOIN / GROUP BY
→ Redshift

11. Snapshot

Snapshot = backup of Redshift cluster at a point in time.

Redshift Cluster
↓
Snapshot
↓
Backup

Snapshots are incremental: Headshot = full data

Snapshot sau = saves only the changes

12. Restore Snapshot

Restore does not overwrite the old cluster.

Snapshot
↓
Restore
↓
New Redshift Cluster

Restore Snapshot = create new cluster.

13. Automated Snapshot

Redshift automatically creates backups periodically.

Cluster
↓
Automated Snapshot
↓
Retention Window

Used for: Regular Recovery · Accidental Change · Operational Backup

14. Manual Snapshot

You create it yourself:

Redshift
↓
Manual Snapshot

Keep it until you delete it.

Before migration · Before major change · Long-term backup

15. Cross-Region Snapshot

You can copy snapshots to another Region:

Region A
Redshift
↓
Snapshot
↓
Copy
↓
Region B

Disaster Recovery

If Region A has problems:

Snapshot in Region B
↓
Restore
↓
New Cluster

16. Multi-AZ

Redshift supports Multi-AZ for suitable configurations.

AZ A
+
AZ B
↓
Redshift

High Availability

Multi-AZ ≠ Cross-Region DR

Cross-Region still uses its own snapshot copy/DR strategy.

17. QuickSight / Tableau

Redshift often works as a backend for BI:

Redshift
↓
QuickSight / Tableau
↓
Dashboard
Sales
Orders
Products
Customers
↓
Redshift
↓
JOIN / Aggregate
↓
QuickSight
↓
Business Dashboard

Use cases:

RDS / S3
↓
ETL
↓
Redshift
↓
QuickSight
Sales + Product + Customer
↓
Redshift
↓
Large JOIN / Aggregate
↓

↓
Automated Snapshot
↓
Recovery

↓

19. Redshift Spectrum & Comparison of Provisioned vs Serverless for Data Services

Redshift Spectrum: Use Redshift's computing power to directly query SQL data on S3 (join immediately with Redshift internal tables) without having to first load the entire file into the cluster; If only simple ad-hoc queries on S3 do not have Redshift available, priority is given Athena.

Data servicesProvisioned modeServerless mode
Amazon RedshiftManage the type/number of nodes in the clusterSelf-scaling data warehouse capacity accordingly RPU
Amazon OpenSearchChoose your own domain instances and EBSSelf-scaling search/vector according to OCU
Amazon MSK (Kafka)Choose the number of brokers and disk capacitySelf-scaling throughput streaming, without managing brokers
AWS DMSChoose your own replication instance sizeSelf-scaling between min/max DCU for Full Load & CDC
Quick review · SAA-C03

Redshift = data warehouse large-scale OLAP analytics, not the default choice for OLTP transactions.

Amazon OpenSearch Service

Purpose: Amazon OpenSearch Service is a managed search + analytics service, used to create a search index to help search quickly and flexibly across many fields.

OpenSearch = Search Engine + Log Analytics + Dashboard

Database keeps original data, OpenSearch keeps index for quick search.

1. What is OpenSearch used for?

Product examples include: Name · Description · Category · Tags · Brand

User search: "iphone pro"

OpenSearch can: Full-text Search · Partial Match · Filter · Sort · Ranking

For example:

"iph pro"
↓
OpenSearch
↓
iPhone 15 Pro
iPhone 16 Pro

2. Database vs OpenSearch

DynamoDB example: DynamoDB = source of truth = saves real data

OpenSearch: OpenSearch = search index = search service

Application
↓
DynamoDB
↓
Streams
↓
Lambda
↓
OpenSearch

Khi user:

Get item by ID
→ DynamoDB
Search by Name / Description / Tag
→ OpenSearch

3. Why not just use DynamoDB?

DynamoDB is strong when querying with: Partition Key · Sort Key · Secondary Index

But if needed: Search by text · Partial Match · Autocomplete · Search multiple fields

then OpenSearch is more suitable.

ProductId = 123
→ DynamoDB
"wireless noise cancelling headphone"
→ OpenSearch

4. Eventual Consistency

Because OpenSearch is usually an index copied from the database:

DynamoDB Update
↓
Streams
↓
Lambda
↓
OpenSearch Update

so there may be a small delay.

Database = latest data

OpenSearch = updates may be a little slower

Here it is eventual consistency of search index.

5. Log Analytics

OpenSearch is also very popular for searching logs.

Application Logs
↓
CloudWatch Logs
↓
OpenSearch
↓
Search / Filter / Dashboard

There are 2 popular flows:

CloudWatch Logs
↓
Subscription Filter
↓
Lambda
↓
OpenSearch

or:

CloudWatch Logs
↓
Subscription Filter
↓
Firehose
↓
OpenSearch

6. Lambda vs Firehose Ingest

  • Lambda = flexible transform log = more custom logic
  • Firehose = managed delivery = buffering = less self-management
  • Remember: Lambda path = event-driven / custom transform
  • Firehose path = managed near real-time delivery

7. OpenSearch Dashboards

OpenSearch has a visualization interface:

Indexed Data
↓
OpenSearch Dashboards
↓
Chart / Graph / Search
Error Logs
↓
OpenSearch
↓
Dashboard:
Error Count
Top IP
Latency
Status Code

8. Managed vs Serverless

  • There are 2 types of deployment: Provisioned OpenSearch = you choose cluster capacity
  • OpenSearch Serverless = AWS manages scaling/capacity more
  • Provisioned = more control over the cluster
  • Serverless = less infrastructure management

9. Security

OpenSearch can use: IAM · VPC / Network Policy · TLS · KMS Encryption · Cognito for Dashboard login

User
↓
Cognito
↓
OpenSearch Dashboards

While: IAM = control API access

10. OpenSearch is not a transactional database

OpenSearch should not be used as a source of truth for:

Payment · Order Transaction · Account Balance

Should: RDS / DynamoDB = save standard data

and: OpenSearch = search / analytics

11. Use Cases

Quick review · SAA-C03

OpenSearch is used for search, log analytics and indexing; not a primary relational data source instead of RDS.

Amazon EMR

Purpose: Amazon EMR is a managed service for running big-data processing clusters across multiple EC2 instances.

EMR = Spark / Hadoop / HBase / Presto / Flink = big data processing in a distributed fashion

AWS takes care of basic provisioning/configuration; You still choose the framework, nodes, and how to optimize your workload.

1. Node in EMR

  • Primary = cluster coordination = health management / job schedule
  • Core = run task = save data in cluster
  • Task = only runs task = does not keep data
Primary
↓ divide job into multiple tasks
Core + Task Nodes
↓
parallel processing
↓
Output

Primary dispatcher, Core = compute + data, Task = compute only.

2. Spot / On-Demand

On-Demand = stable = not reclaimed like Spot

Spot = cheaper = can be terminated

Spot is often best suited for: Task Nodes

Because the Task node does not hold data and the task can retry.

Primary/Core usually needs more stability.

3. Auto Scaling

EMR can automatically change the number of nodes according to workload:

Job increases
↓
add node

Job decreased
↓
reduce nodes

→ reduce costs when workload changes.

4. Long-running vs Transient Cluster

4.1. Long-running

Cluster is always running

Suitable: Notebook · Interactive workload · HBase · Service needs to operate continuously

4.2. Transient

Create Cluster
↓
Run Job
↓
Write the results to S3
↓
Terminate Cluster

Suitable for: Batch · Periodic ETL · Spark processing

→ avoid paying when cluster is idle.

5. Popular flow

S3/HDFS
↓
EMR
↓
Spark/Hadoop Job
↓
Core + Task parallel processing
↓
S3 / Data Store

Important data should be saved in sustainable storage such as S3, especially when using Spot or transient clusters.

6. EMR vs Athena vs Glue

  • EMR = Spark / Hadoop / Flink = custom big-data code = more cluster control
  • Athena = Ad-hoc SQL directly on S3 = no cluster management
  • Glue = serverless ETL = less infrastructure management

7. Use Cases

Use cases:

Big Data on S3
↓
Spark on EMR
↓
Transform / Feature Engineering
Hadoop / HBase workload
↓
EMR
Large-scale ML preprocessing / Web indexing
↓
EMR Cluster

8. Instance Groups vs Instance Fleets, Managed Scaling & Spark Catalog

Concepts on EMRDistinguishing point
EMR Instance Group vs EMR Instance FleetInstance Group fixes one EC2 type per node group; Instance Fleet Allows mixing many types of EC2 + On-Demand/Spot and manually selecting the available AZ
EMR Managed ScalingAutomatically increase/decrease the number of Core and Task nodes continuously according to the actual load of the job to optimize costs
Apache Spark vs Spark CatalogSpark is a distributed processing engine; spark_catalog (usually integrated Glue Data Catalog) only manages table/schema metadata, not original data
Quick review · SAA-C03

EMR is a big data processing platform using the Spark/Hadoop ecosystem and supported frameworks.

Amazon QuickSight

Purpose: Amazon QuickSight is a serverless Business Intelligence (BI) service for creating:

Charts · Reports · Interactive Analysis · Dashboards

QuickSight is used to visualize data and create insights, not a database, data warehouse or ETL engine.

QuickSight = BI = Visualization = Dashboard

1. Where does QuickSight get its data?

Can connect to: RDS · Aurora · Athena · Redshift · S3 · OpenSearch · Timestream

There are also: CSV / XLSX / JSON / TSV · Salesforce / Jira · On-prem database qua JDBC

Data Source
↓
QuickSight
↓
Charts / Dashboard

2. Analysis vs Dashboard

2.1. Analysis

Analysis = where the author creates and edits dashboards.

Can add: Charts · Filters · Parameters · Controls · SortMORE »

For example:

Sales Analysis
├── Revenue chart
├── Region filter
├── Date filter
└── Product chart

2.2. Dashboard

After analysis is complete:

Analysis
↓
Publish
↓
Dashboard

Dashboards are mainly for viewers.

Analysis = where build/edit

Dashboard = published version to share/view

Viewers can still interact with: Filter · Drill-down · Controls

within published scope.

3. Direct Query

QuickSight can directly query the data source.

QuickSight
↓
Redshift / Athena / RDS

Each time the dashboard needs data:

QuickSight
↓
query source
↓
get results

Advantages: Newer data

Disadvantages: Depends on source speed · Increase source load

4. SPICE

SPICE = QuickSight's in-memory engine.

Data Source
↓
Import
↓
SPICE
↓
Dashboard

QuickSight queries the imported data in memory instead of continuously querying the source.

Benefits: Faster dashboard · Reduced database load

In return:

Data is not automatically always fresh
↓
need refresh

5. Direct Query vs SPICE

Direct Query = query source directly = fresher data = depends on source

SPICE = import copy into QuickSight = faster = need to refresh

Direct Query = prioritize freshness. SPICE = performance priority.

6. Sharing

Dashboards can: Share with QuickSight User · Share with QuickSight Group · Embed into application

Redshift
↓
QuickSight
↓
Sales Dashboard
↓
Embed into the company portal

QuickSight users/groups is the identity within QuickSight.

QuickSight Group ≠ IAM Group

7. Column-Level Security

Enterprise edition supported Column-Level Security (CLS).

Table example: Name · Department · Salary · Email

HR: Sales user: Name · Department · Email

don't see: Salary

CLS = controls which columns the user can view.

8. Row-Level Security

In addition to columns, you can limit by row.

User Singapore
→ only see Region = Singapore

User Vietnam
→ only see Region = Vietnam

RLS = controls which rows the user can see

Remember: CLS = Column

RLS = Row

9. Security Layers

There are two different types of permissions:

QuickSight → Data Source

QuickSight needs permission/credential to read the database.

And:

User → QuickSight Dashboard

QuickSight user/group decides who gets to see the dashboard.

Having permission to view the dashboard does not mean that the user directly has access to the source database.

10. What doesn't QuickSight do?

QuickSight is not: Data Warehouse · ETL Engine · Transactional Database

Glue = transform data

Redshift = warehouse

Athena = query data

QuickSight = visualize results

11. Use Cases

Use cases:

S3
↓
Athena

↓

↓

↓
Operational Dashboard
Dashboard
↓

↓
Web Application
Quick review · SAA-C03

QuickSight serves BI dashboard/visualization; not a data warehouse; typically read from Athena, Redshift, or another source.

AWS Glue & Lake Formation

Purpose: AWS Glue provides serverless ETL and metadata catalog to prepare data for analytics; AWS Lake Formation builds a data lake management layer on Glue to ingest, catalog, and centrally control access to data typically stored in S3.

AWS Glue = ETL + Data Catalog

Lake Formation = Governance + Permission for Data Lake

S3 = contains data

Glue = learn data + transform data

Lake Formation = control who can see the data

1. AWS Glue

Glue is the service serverless ETL.

Extract
↓
Transform
↓
Load

For example:

Raw CSV/JSON on S3
↓
Glue
↓
Clean + Convert Parquet + Partition
↓
Curated Data on S3
↓
Athena / Redshift / QuickSight

2. Glue Crawler

Crawler = explore data and schema.

It reads: S3 · RDS · DynamoDB · JDBC Source

then discovered: Column · Data Type · Partition · Location

then save the metadata to: Glue Data Catalog

Crawler only discovers schema, does not transform data.

3. Glue Data Catalog

Data Catalog is like table of contents of data lake.

Glue Data Catalog = Table + Schema + Partition + S3 Location

Table: orders · Columns: id, price, date · Location: s3://data/orders/

The real data is still located at: S3

The catalog only holds: Metadata

Services using Catalog: Athena · Redshift Spectrum · EMR · Glue

4. Crawler vs Glue Job

Crawler = What structure does this data have?

Glue Job = How to transform data?

S3 Raw CSV
↓
Crawler
↓
Catalog knows schema

Then:

Raw CSV
↓
Glue Job
↓
Clean / Join / Filter
↓
Parquet

5. Job Bookmark

Job Bookmark remembers which data has been processed.

Run 1
→ process files A and B

Run 2
→ only processes C files

Instead:

A + B + C
→ reprocess everything

Bookmark = avoid reprocessing old data.

It should not be understood as an exactly-once guarantee for every pipeline.

6. Glue Studio & DataBrew

Glue Studio = GUI create/run/monitor Glue Jobs

DataBrew = low-code/no-code data cleaning

DataBrew is suitable for: Remove duplicate · Normalize · Clean values · Standardize data

7. Glue Streaming ETL

In addition to batch ETL, Glue can handle streams.

Kinesis / Kafka / MSK
↓
Glue Streaming ETL
↓
Transform
↓
S3 / Analytics Target

It uses: Apache Spark Structured Streaming

8. AWS Lake Formation

Lake Formation is used to manage and protect Data Lake.

Lake Formation = Data Lake Governance

Usually data is still located in: Amazon S3

Lake Formation is not new storage.

9. Glue vs Lake Formation

Glue = data has what schema? = transform data how?

Lake Formation = Who can see which data?

S3 Data Lake
↓
Glue Catalog
↓
Lake Formation Permissions
↓
Athena
↓
User

10. Fine-Grained Access

Lake Formation can limit permissions to:

Database · Table · Column · Row

Table example:

Employee
├── Name
├── Department
└── Salary

HR: Name ✅ · Department ✅ · Salary ✅

Analyst: Name ✅ · Department ✅ · Salary ❌

Column-Level Access

Rows can be limited:

Vietnam Team
→ only see Region = Vietnam

11. Lake Formation does not replace IAM/S3/KMS

Lake Formation is an additional layer of governance.

You may still need: IAM · S3 permissions · KMS permissions · Network access

Example file using SSE-KMS:

User
↓
Lake Formation allows tables
↓
but no KMS permission
↓
Still can't read data

12. Full Flow Data Lake

S3 / RDS / DynamoDB
↓

↓

↓

↓

Streaming platforms: Amazon MSK & Managed Service for Apache Flink

Purpose: Amazon MSK is managed Apache Kafka to stream event streams by topic/partition; Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink (formerly Kinesis Data Analytics for Apache Flink) runs Flink managed applications to process real-time streams from Kinesis Data Streams or MSK.

Amazon MSK = Managed Apache Kafka = receive and transmit event stream

Managed Service for Apache Flink = real-time stream processing

Producer
↓
MSK / Kafka
↓
Flink
↓
Transform / Aggregate / Detect
↓
Sink

1. Amazon MSK

Amazon MSK is Apache Kafka is managed by AWS.

Instead of self: Install Kafka · Manage broker · Patch · Recovery · Scaling

AWS manages the infrastructure for you.

MSK = Kafka API + Kafka ecosystem + Managed infrastructure

Clusters usually run in VPC and can span multiple AZs to increase HA.

2. Kafka Model

  • Kafka has 3 important components: Producer = send event
  • Topic = stream / event type
  • Consumer = read event

For example:

Order Service
↓ Producer
Topic: orders
↓
Fraud Consumer
Analytics Consumer
Notification Consumer

An event stream can be processed independently by multiple consumers.

3. Topic & Partition

Topics are divided into: Partitions

orders topic
├── Partition 0
├── Partition 1
└── Partition 2

Partition helps: Parallel Processing · Scale throughput

Consumer Group can divide partitions among multiple consumers:

Partition 0 → Consumer A
Partition 1 → Consumer B
Partition 2 → Consumer C

Topic = logical stream, Partition = parallelism unit.

Kafka can add partition, but does not reduce partition count directly.

4. Producer & Consumer is still your code

MSK manages the Kafka infrastructure, but the business logic is still handled by the application.

Producer Code
↓
MSK
↓
Consumer Code
↓
Business Logic

MSK does not understand: Order · Payment · Fraud · Customer

it just transports/saves event.

5. Storage & Replication

Kafka records are saved according to retention:

Producer
↓
Broker
↓
EBS Storage

Broker can replicate data:

Broker A
↓ replicate
Broker B
↓
Broker C

→ increase durability and availability.

6. MSK Serverless

Two main ways: Provisioned MSK = select/manage broker capacity

MSK Serverless = AWS self-provisioning + scale capacity

Serverless is suitable when: Traffic changes a lot · Don't want capacity planning

Provisioned is suitable when more cluster/capacity control is needed.

7. Managed Service for Apache Flink

Flink is used for Process data while the stream is running.

Kinesis / MSK
↓
Flink
↓
Transform
Filter
Aggregate
Join
Window
↓
Output

Can be written with: Java · Scala · SQL

8. Flink example

Event:

User A → $100
User A → $500
User A → $3000

Flink can:

Group by User
↓
total in 5 minutes
↓
Abnormal detection
↓
Fraud Alert

Here it is stateful stream processing.

9. Flink State & Checkpoint

Flink can remember state between events.

User A total = $600

New event: +$3000

Flink continues from the old state: Total = $3600

To recover:

State
↓
Checkpoint / Snapshot
↓
Failure
↓
Restore State

Checkpoint = saves state so Flink can continue after failure.

10. Auto Scaling & Parallelism

Flink can parallelize:

Stream
↓
Worker 1
Worker 2
Worker 3

When throughput increases:

Auto Scaling
↓
More Compute

But correctness still depends: Checkpoint · Offsets · Duplicate handling · Late events · Sink idempotency

11. Where does Flink read from?

According to slide content: Kinesis Data Streams ✅ · Amazon MSK ✅ · Data Firehose ❌

Remember: KDS / MSK = stream source

Flink = stream processor

12. Kinesis Data Streams vs MSK

Kinesis Data Streams = AWS-native streaming = uses Shards

MSK = Apache Kafka = use Topics + Partitions

Kinesis
→ want AWS-native, less interested in Kafka ecosystem
MSK
→ app used Kafka
→ needs Kafka APIs / ecosystem

13. Shard vs Partition

Kinesis = Shard

Kafka / MSK = Partition

Both are used to: chia workload + parallel processing

And we all need to choose a reasonable partition key to avoid:

Hot Partition / Hot Shard

14. MSK Consumer

MSK can be consumed by: Managed Flink · Glue Streaming ETL · Lambda · EC2 / ECS / EKS Application

Application
↓
MSK
├── Flink → Real-time Analytics
├── Lambda → Business Logic
└── Glue → S3 Data Lake

15. Use Cases

Use cases:

Microservices
↓
MSK
↓
many independent consumers
Kinesis / MSK
↓
Flink
↓
Fraud Detection
Clickstream
↓
Flink
↓
5-minute aggregation
↓
Analytics
Variable Kafka Traffic
↓
MSK Serverless
Quick review · SAA-C03

MSK manages Kafka; Managed Service for Apache Flink handles streaming according to state/event time. Choose according to ingest or real-time compute purposes.

Serverless big-data ingestion pipeline

Purpose: This is a pattern to receive real-time events → save to S3 → query with Athena → create dashboard/report without having to manage the ingestion cluster yourself.

IoT Devices
↓
IoT Core
↓
Kinesis Data Streams
↓
Data Firehose
↓
S3
↓
Athena
↓
QuickSight / Redshift

1. Role of each service

  • IoT Core = receive events from IoT devices
  • Kinesis Data Streams = streaming ingestion real-time
  • Data Firehose: buffer + delivery managed → usually writes to S3
  • S3 = save raw / processed data
  • Athena = SQL query on S3
  • QuickSight = dashboard / visualization
  • Redshift = warehouse analytics if large queries/joins are needed frequently

2. Full flow

IoT Devices
↓
IoT Core
↓
Kinesis Data Streams
↓
Data Firehose
↓
Lambda Transform (optional)
↓
S3 Ingestion Bucket
↓
SQS (optional)
↓
Lambda
↓
Athena Query
↓
S3 Reporting Bucket
↓
QuickSight / Redshift

3. Kinesis Data Streams

KDS receives data real-time.

Devices / Applications
↓
Kinesis Data Streams
↓
continuous records

For example:

Temperature event · Click event · Application log · Telemetry

Need to choose: Partition Key

reasonable to avoid hot shards.

4. Data Firehose

Firehose took the stream buffer and delivery to destination.

Kinesis Data Streams
↓
Firehose
↓
S3

Firehose can: Buffer records · Batch records · Retry delivery · Transform using Lambda

Because there is buffering: Firehose = Near Real-Time

Not every event that arrives immediately appears in S3.

5. Lambda Transform in Firehose

Can add:

Firehose
↓
Lambda
↓
Transform Record
↓
S3
Raw JSON
↓
Clean / Add field / Change format
↓
S3

Lambda here is optional.

6. S3 + SQS + Lambda

When Firehose writes the file to S3:

S3 Event
↓
SQS
↓
Lambda

SQS is used to: Buffer · Retry · Decouple · Handle traffic spike

If the pipeline is simple:

S3
↓
Lambda

also.

7. Why add SQS?

Without SQS:

S3 Event
↓
Lambda

Yes SQS:

S3 Event
↓
SQS Queue
↓
Lambda

If Lambda is slow:

Message
→ wait in SQS

instead of applying pressure directly to Lambda.

SQS = buffer + decouple + retry.

8. SQS does not guarantee exactly-once

Message/event can be reprocessed.

Same Event
↓
Lambda
↓
can run multiple times

So consumers should: Idempotent

For example, the same file is processed again: Do not create duplicate reports · Do not insert duplicate data

9. Athena Query

Lambda can trigger Athena:

Lambda
↓
Start Athena Query
↓
Athena reads data from S3
↓
SQL

SELECT device_id, AVG(temperature) · FROM telemetry · GROUP BY device_id;

Athena saves the result to: S3 Reporting Bucket

10. Query Result is in S3

Athena
↓
Query Result
↓
S3

Then:

QuickSight
↓
Dashboard

or:

Reporting Data
↓
Redshift
↓
Warehouse Analytics

11. Real-Time vs Near Real-Time

Kinesis Data Streams = Real-Time Streaming

Firehose = Buffer + Delivery = Near Real-Time

Event occurs
↓
KDS received it immediately
↓
Firehose buffer
↓
Then write the batch to S3

Therefore, the entire pipeline to the dashboard is usually not strict real-time.

12. QuickSight vs Redshift at the end of the pipeline

Athena / S3
↓
QuickSight

Use when: Dashboard · Reporting · Visualization

If needed: many JOINs · large aggregates · continuously repeated queries

then:

S3
↓
Redshift
↓
QuickSight

may be more suitable.

13. Use Cases

Use cases:

IoT Devices
↓
Kinesis
↓
S3
↓
Athena
↓
QuickSight

→ IoT telemetry dashboard.

Application Logs
↓
Kinesis
↓
Firehose
↓
S3
↓
Athena

→ log analytics.

Clickstream
↓
S3
↓
Athena
↓
Redshift
↓
BI

→ big analytics/reporting.

Quick review · SAA-C03

Serverless ingest flows typically separate collection, storage, ETL, and query; Select Kinesis/Firehose, S3, Glue, Athena by role.

AWS Data Pipeline, Amazon MWAA & AWS Data Exchange

Purpose: Orchestrate complex scheduled/dependent data processing workflows and integrate third-party data sets to AWS.

1. Amazon MWAA vs AWS Data Pipeline

ServiceRoles & Distinguishing Points
Amazon MWAA (Managed Workflows for Apache Airflow)Run environment Apache Airflow managed to orchestrate multi-step data pipelines using Python code (DAGs), handling dependencies, retries, and warnings; MWAA itself coordination only, while computation/storage is handled by S3, Glue, EMR, Redshift
AWS Data PipelineService that coordinates periodic data migration and transformation between AWS/on-premises data warehouses according to dependencies
Amazon MWAA (scheduled DAG) → enable AWS Glue ETL read S3 → load Amazon Redshift table → send SNS notification

2. AWS Data Exchange

AWS Data Exchange: data marketplace helps subscribe to commercial/public datasets from third-party providers and receive them directly into S3, Redshift, Lake Formation or via API without having to write your own collection pipeline.

Amazon Lex & Amazon Connect

Purpose: Amazon Lex is used to create chatbots/voice bots that understand user intentions, while Amazon Connect is a cloud contact center to receive calls and coordinate contact flow.

Lex = understand speech/text = recognize intent

ASR
= Speech → Text

NLU
= understand what the user wants to do

For example:

↓
Lex
↓
Intent = BookAppointment

Intent = user intent, not just keywords.

Lex is suitable for: Chatbot · Voice Bot · Call Center Bot

2. Amazon Connect

Amazon Connect is cloud contact center.

Caller
↓
Amazon Connect
↓
Contact Flow
↓
Bot / Agent / Backend

→ Intent = BookAppointment

↓

Lex confidence is low
or
case has not been designed yet
↓
Fallback
↓
Human Agent

Backend still needs: Validate input · Authorization · Business Rules · Idempotency

Because Lex only understands the intent, it cannot decide whether to change the data or not.

Amazon SageMaker AI

Purpose: Amazon SageMaker AI is a managed service to build, train, tune, and deploy custom Machine Learning models.

SageMaker = Build ML Model = Train = Tune = Deploy = Predict

Different from available AI services such as Translate or Textract:

Translate / Textract = ready-made AWS model

SageMaker = you train your own model for your own problem

1. Basic ML Flow

Historical Data
↓
Features + Labels
↓
SageMaker Training
↓
Model
↓
New Data
↓
Prediction

For example:

Historical Students
↓
Study Time + Experience
↓
Exam Result
↓
Train Model
↓
New Student
↓
Predict Pass / Fail

2. Training Data

Training usually requires: Features = input information

Label = result needs model to learn

Study Hours
AWS Experience
IT Experience
↓
Features

Exam Score
↓
Label

The model learns the relationship between features and labels to predict new data.

3. Train / Tune / Deploy

  • Train = model learned from historical data
  • Tune = try configurations to get a better model
  • Deploy = send the model to the endpoint for the application to call
Train
↓
Tune
↓
Model Artifact
↓
Deploy Endpoint
↓
Application
↓
Prediction

4. Managed but still have to check the model

SageMaker manages the infrastructure, but the model still depends on:

Training Data · Feature · Label · Model Quality

If data is wrong:

Bad Data
↓
Bad Model
↓
Bad Prediction

Also need monitor: Model Quality · Data Drift · Prediction Quality

5. Use Cases

Use cases:

Customer History
↓
SageMaker
↓
Churn Prediction
Transaction Data
↓
SageMaker
↓
Fraud Risk Score
Sales History
↓
SageMaker
↓
Demand Forecast

6. SageMaker Batch Transform vs Real-Time Endpoint

Inference modeMechanisms & Use Cases
SageMaker Batch TransformRun offline batch inference on large data sets stored in S3 and then write the results to S3, automatically turn off compute when the job is finished — used when immediate results are not needed for each request (not used for ETL data cleaning)
SageMaker Real-Time EndpointMaintain a continuously running HTTPS endpoint to deliver predictable low latency (milliseconds) for each online request
Quick review · SAA-C03

SageMaker AI is a machine learning lifecycle management platform from data preparation/training to deployment.

Monitoring, Audit & Observability

Amazon CloudWatch: metrics, logs, alarms & insights

Purpose: Amazon CloudWatch is used to monitor the AWS system with metrics, logs, dashboards and alarms.

CloudWatch = Metrics + Logs + Dashboard + Alarms

It replied: "How is the system running?"

Other than: CloudTrail = who called which AWS API?

1. Functional groups and EventBridge

Functional groupWhat to use?
MetricsCollect measurements over time: CPU, latency, request count
LogsCentralize application/service logs
DashboardsDisplays graphs and system status
AlarmsMonitor conditions/status for alerts or action calls
InsightsAnalyze logs and observe specialized workloads such as containers/Lambda
CloudWatch Events → Amazon EventBridgeReceive events, use filtering rules and pass to targets like Lambda/SQS

These are functional groups, not mutually exclusive types of monitoring. CloudWatch Events is the former name of Amazon EventBridge; Alarm tracks metrics, EventBridge routes events such as EC2 state changes or state change alarms.

2. CloudWatch Metrics

Metric = a value measured over time.

For example:

CPUUtilization · NetworkIn · RequestCount · ErrorCount

EC2 / Lambda / RDS...
↓
Metrics
↓
CloudWatch
↓
Dashboard / Alarm

3. Namespace & Dimension

3.1. Namespace

Used to group metrics by service/application.

AWS/EC2 · AWS/Lambda · AWS/RDS

3.2. Dimension

Used to determine which resource the metric belongs to.

Metric = CPUUtilization

Dimension = InstanceId = i-123456

→ Same as CPUUtilization but each EC2 has its own time series.

4. EC2 granularity and RDS metrics

Resources/modeGranularityData
EC2 Basic Monitoring5 minutes for most metrics; status checks 1 minuteEC2 metrics default
EC2 Detailed Monitoring1 minuteEC2 metrics; Need to turn on, there is a fee
Default RDS CloudWatch1 minuteDatabase metrics; No need for EC2 Detailed Monitoring
RDS Enhanced MonitoringCan be down to 1 secondOS/process metrics → CloudWatch Logs; Need to enable and monitor role

EC2 RAM needs Agent/custom metric. Enhanced Monitoring collection 1 second does not mean the RDS console refreshes every second. ·

5. Custom Metrics

You can manually send metrics to CloudWatch.

Important examples:

Default EC2
→ has CPU / Network...

RAM Usage
→ no default
→ need Agent / Custom Metric

6. CloudWatch Dashboard

Metrics / Logs
↓
CloudWatch Dashboard
↓
Graph / Chart / Status

Dashboard example: CPU · Memory · Request Count · Errors · Latency

7. CloudWatch Logs

CloudWatch Logs is used to centralize logs from application/service.

Popular sources: Lambda · ECS · API Gateway · VPC Flow Logs · CloudTrail · EC2 · On-premises

7.1. EC2 / On-premises

Logs are not automatically sent to CloudWatch.

EC2
↓
CloudWatch Agent
↓
CloudWatch Logs

Agents need: Config + IAM Permission

8. CloudWatch Logs Insights

Logs Insights = query/search log saved in CloudWatch Logs.

CloudWatch Logs
↓
Logs Insights
↓
Filter / Search / Aggregate

Find ERROR · Count requests · Find IP · View latest log

Query example: fields @timestamp, @message · | sort @timestamp desc · | limit 20

Logs Insights = historical query log, not streaming engine.

9. Put Logs out

There are 2 easy ways to remember.

9.1. S3 Export

CloudWatch Logs
↓
Export Task
↓
S3

= Batch = not for real-time

9.2. Subscription Filter

CloudWatch Logs
↓
Subscription Filter
↓
Kinesis / Firehose / Lambda

= near real-time

CloudWatch Logs
↓
Subscription Filter
↓
Firehose
↓
S3 / OpenSearch

10. CloudWatch Agent

10.1. Logs Agent

Old Agent = only sends logs

10.2. Unified Agent

Unified Agent = Logs + System Metrics

You can get more: RAM · Disk · Processes · Swap · Network · Detailed CPU

EC2
↓
Unified Agent
↓
RAM + Disk + Logs
↓
CloudWatch

11. Metric Streams

Metric Streams are used to send metrics continuously to other systems.

CloudWatch Metrics
↓
Metric Stream
↓
Data Firehose
↓
S3 / Redshift / OpenSearch

Or:

CloudWatch
↓
Datadog / New Relic / Splunk...

Metric Stream = stream metrics out in near real-time.

12. CloudWatch Alarms

Alarm monitors a metric and triggers action when a threshold is exceeded.

Metric
↓
CloudWatch Alarm
↓
Threshold is overtaken
↓
Action
CPU > 80%
↓
ALARM
↓
SNS Notification

Alarm has 3 states: OK · ALARM · INSUFFICIENT_DATA

Actions can be: SNS · Auto Scaling · EC2 Stop · EC2 Reboot · EC2 Recover

13. Composite Alarm

Composite Alarm combines multiple alarms.

CPU Alarm
AND
Disk IOPS Alarm
↓
Composite Alarm
↓
Incident

Purpose:

Reduce alarm noise

Composite Alarm does not measure new metrics, it only looks at the status of other alarms.

14. Logs → Metric → Alarm

Logs can be turned into metrics.

Application Logs
↓
Metric Filter
↓
ERROR Count Metric
↓
Alarm
↓
SNS

15. EC2 Status Check & Recovery

  • EC2 has status checks such as: Instance Status = problem inside VM
  • System Status = underlying problem AWS host
  • EBS Status = attached storage problem
StatusCheckFailed_System
↓
CloudWatch Alarm
↓
EC2 Recover

→ AWS moves the instance to more powerful hardware.

16. CloudWatch Insights

  • Some specialized features: Container Insights = monitor ECS / EKS / Containers
  • Lambda Insights = detailed Lambda monitor
  • Contributor Insights = find top contributors like the IP sending the most traffic/errors
  • Application Insights = monitor application and dependency

No need to remember too much, just remember that these are specialized monitoring tools of CloudWatch.

17. Network Monitor

Used to monitor the network between AWS and on-premises.

AWS · ↕ · Direct Connect / VPN · ↕ · On-premises

Follow: Latency · Packet Loss · Jitter

18. Overall flow

AWS Resources / Application
↓
Metrics + Logs
↓
CloudWatch
↓
Dashboard / Logs Insights
↓
Alarm
↓
SNS / Auto Scaling / EC2 Action

19. Use Cases

Use cases:

EC2
↓
Unified Agent
↓
RAM / Disk / Logs
↓
CloudWatch
↓
Alarm
Application Logs
↓
CloudWatch Logs
↓
Logs Insights
↓
Troubleshooting
Logs
↓
Subscription Filter
↓

↓
OpenSearch
CPU + IOPS Alarm
↓

↓

20. Alarm Actions, Dashboard Sharing, Cross-Account OAM & Distinguishing Access Logs

CloudWatch featureMechanism of action
CloudWatch Alarm ActionsActivate SNS, Auto Scaling, Lambda, EC2 Actions (Reboot, Recover Go to a new physical host to keep the same IP/EBS when hardware fails, Stop, Terminate) and create SSM OpsItem / Incident
CloudWatch Dashboard SharingShare dashboard to outsiders via public link, specified email/password or SSO without creating an IAM User in the account (Logs widgets are hidden by default unless permissions are enabled)
CloudWatch Cross-Account OAM (Observability)Create OAM Sink at Monitoring Account and deploy OAM Link (automation via CloudFormation StackSets) in Source Accounts to centralize Metrics, Logs and Traces
ServiceLog TypeRecorded content
CloudTrailAPI Activity LogsWho calls which AWS API, when, from which IP
VPCVPC Flow LogsIP packet metadata (Source/Dest IP, Port, Protocol, ACCEPT/REJECT — does not contain HTTP body)
ALB / NLB / API Gateway / CloudFrontAccess LogsHTTP/HTTPS request details or TLS connection at the distribution/load balancing layer
S3Server Access LogsDetails of each object-level access request to the bucket
AWS WAFWAF LogsWeb traffic that matches the Allow, Block or Count rule
Quick review · SAA-C03

CloudWatch monitors metrics/logs/alarms; CloudTrail logs API activity; Config tracks configuration and compliance.

Amazon EventBridge

Purpose: Amazon EventBridge is a serverless event bus used to receive events, filter events, and automatically send events to other services.

EventBridge = receive event + filter event + route event to target

"Something happened"
↓
EventBridge detected
↓
"Then do something"

1. Basic Flow

Event Source
↓
EventBridge Bus
↓
Rule
↓
Target

For example:

EC2 is stopped
↓
EventBridge
↓
Rule match
↓
Lambda / SNS / SQS

Rule decides when to run, Target is the service that actually works.

2. Event Source

EventBridge can receive events from: AWS Services · SaaS Applications · Custom Applications · CloudTrail

EC2 state change · S3 event · CodeBuild failed · CloudTrail API call · Custom application event

3. Rule

Rules have 2 main types:

3.1. Event Pattern

Runs when an event meets the conditions.

Event occurs
↓
Rule checks JSON
↓
Match?
↓
Target
EC2 State = stopped
↓
EventBridge Rule
↓
SNS Alert

3.2. Schedule / Cron

Run over time.

Every 1 hour
↓
EventBridge
↓
Lambda
00:00 every day
↓
EventBridge
↓
Lambda cleanup temporary files

4. Target

Target is the service that handles events after the rule match.

Could be: Lambda · SQS · SNS · Step Functions · ECS · AWS Batch · Kinesis · CodePipeline · CodeBuild · SSM

Order Created
↓
EventBridge
↓
SQS
↓
Order Worker

5. Event Bus

  • EventBridge has the following bus types: Default Event Bus = AWS service events
  • Partner Event Bus = SaaS like Datadog/Zendesk
  • Custom Event Bus = event from your application
My Application
↓
Custom Event Bus
↓
Rule
↓
Lambda

6. Producer & Consumer separate

EventBridge key points:

Producer
↓
EventBridge
↓
Consumer

The producer does not need to know who handles the event.

Order Service
↓
"OrderCreated"
↓
EventBridge
├── Inventory Lambda
├── Email SQS
└── Analytics Kinesis

Order Service only broadcasts events, no need to call each service directly.

7. CloudTrail + EventBridge

CloudTrail records AWS API activity.

EventBridge can react to those events.

User calls DeleteTable
↓
CloudTrail records
↓
EventBridge Rule match
↓
SNS
↓
Security Team

Or:

Root Login
↓
EventBridge
↓
SNS Alert

EventBridge react after the event occurs, do not block previous API calls.

8. Archive & Replay

EventBridge can save events.

Event Bus
↓
Archive

Then:

Archive
↓
Replay
↓
Rule / Target

Use when: Retest consumer · Reprocess event · Debug incident

Replay does not replace consumer idempotency/retry logic.

9. Schema Registry

Schema Registry = saves/understands the event JSON structure

Event example:

{
  "orderId": "123",
  "status": "PAID"
}

Schema helps the application know what fields the event has.

10. Cross-account

EventBridge can send events between AWS accounts.

Account A
↓
EventBridge
↓
Account B Central Event Bus

Suitable for: Multi-account AWS Organization · Central Monitoring · Central Security Automation

Need: IAM Permission + Event Bus Resource Policy

11. Use Cases

Use cases:

Every 1 hour
↓
EventBridge
↓
Lambda
EC2 State Change
↓
EventBridge
↓
SNS Alert
CloudTrail DeleteTable
↓
EventBridge
↓
Security Notification
OrderCreated
↓
EventBridge
↓
SQS / Lambda / Step Functions
Quick review · SAA-C03

EventBridge receives, filters, and routes events; Scheduler is used to run schedules independently of the source event.

AWS CloudTrail

Purpose: AWS CloudTrail is used to audit activity in an AWS account.

It replied: Who? · Doing what? · When? · From where?

For example:

EC2 is terminated
↓
CloudTrail
↓
Which User/Role calls the API?
Which IP?
What time?
Which API?

CloudTrail = audit AWS API activity.

1. CloudTrail vs CloudWatch

CloudTrail = who did what on AWS?

CloudWatch = How is the system working?

EC2 is deleted
→ CloudTrail

EC2 CPU = 95%
→ CloudWatch

2. What does CloudTrail record?

CloudTrail records activity from: AWS Console · CLI · SDK · AWS Services · IAM User / Role

CreateSubnet · DeleteBucket · TerminateInstances · AttachRolePolicy

3. Event History

CloudTrail does Event History default for management events.

Event History = see recent activity = keep for about 90 days

If you need to save longer:

CloudTrail Trail
↓
S3
↓
Athena query

4. Trail

Trail = configure CloudTrail to send long-term logs to storage.

CloudTrail
↓
Trail
↓
S3

You can send more to: CloudWatch Logs

Trails can: Single Region · or · All Regions

5. Types of Events

5.1. Management Events

Is an AWS resource management operation.

CreateSubnet · AttachRolePolicy · CreateTrail · TerminateInstances

Can be divided: Read = read only

Write = change resources

Management events are the most common type when investigating who edited/deleted a resource.

6. Data Events

Is an activity directly on data/object/function.

S3 GetObject · S3 PutObject · S3 DeleteObject · Lambda Invoke

Data events: High volume · Higher cost · No default logging

So it's usually only enabled for important resources.

7. CloudTrail Insights

CloudTrail Insights is used for discovery unusual activity.

Normal:
10 API calls/minute

Suddenly:
1000 API calls/minute
↓
CloudTrail Insights
↓
Anomaly

Can detect: Abnormal IAM activity · Abnormal Provisioning · API burst · Behavior different from baseline

Management Write Events
↓
CloudTrail Insights
↓
Detect anomaly
↓
EventBridge / Alert

8. Investigation with S3 + Athena

If saving CloudTrail to S3:

CloudTrail
↓
S3
↓
Athena
↓
SQL Query

Example you want to find: Who deletes bucket? · Who terminates EC2? · Who edits Security Group?

9. CloudTrail + EventBridge

CloudTrail logs events.

EventBridge can react to that event.

User calls DeleteTable
↓
CloudTrail records
↓
EventBridge matches
↓
SNS/Lambda/SQS

CloudTrail = record

EventBridge = response

10. Overall flow

User / Role / AWS Service
↓
API Call
↓
CloudTrail
↓
Management / Data / Insights Event
↓
Event History / S3 / CloudWatch Logs
↓
Athena / EventBridge
↓
Investigation / Alert / Automation

11. Use Cases

Use cases:

EC2 is terminated
↓
CloudTrail
↓
Find user + API + IP + time
Sensitive S3 Bucket
↓
Enable Data Events
↓
Log GetObject / PutObject / DeleteObject
CloudTrail
↓
S3
↓
Athena
↓
Long-term audit
Sensitive API Call
↓
CloudTrail
↓
EventBridge
↓
SNS Alert
Quick review · SAA-C03

CloudTrail records AWS API activity for auditing; Event History, trail and CloudTrail Lake have different scope/storage.

AWS Config & compliance automation

Purpose: AWS Config is used to monitor the configuration of AWS resources over time and check whether the resource complies with the rules or not.

AWS Config = Configuration History + Compliance Check

It replied: How are resources configured? · What was the previous configuration like? · Is it compliant with the rule?

1. Easy to understand example

You have rule: Security Group · do not open SSH 22 for 0.0.0.0/0

If there is SG: Port 22 · Source = 0.0.0.0/0

Config rating: NON_COMPLIANT ❌

If only for corporate IP: Port 22 · Source = Company IP

→

COMPLIANT ✅

Config = check if the configuration has the correct policy.

2. What does AWS Config track?

For example:

Security Group · S3 Bucket · EC2 · EBS · ALB · IAM · ...

May know: Current configuration · Previous configuration · When the configuration changed · Compliance status

3. Resource Timeline

Config saves the resource's change history.

10:00 · S3 Bucket = Private · 11:00 · S3 Bucket = Public · 12:00 · S3 Bucket = Private

Config Timeline helps view: When do resources change · Configuration before/after · How compliance changes

Can link to CloudTrail to see which API call caused the change.

4. Config Rules

Rule is used to check configuration.

S3 Buckets
→ cannot be Public

Security Group
→ Do not open SSH all over the Internet

ALB
→ must have SSL Certificate

There are 2 types: AWS Managed Rule = Ready-made AWS

Custom Rule = your own logic = usually use Lambda

5. When does the rule run?

Can run:

Configuration Change
↓
Config evaluate immediately

or:

According to Schedule
↓
Config checked periodically
EC2 configuration changes
↓
Config Rule
↓
COMPLIANT / NON_COMPLIANT

6. Config DOES NOT block changes

Very important point:

User opens S3 Public
↓
Action happened first
↓
Config detected
↓
NON_COMPLIANT

Config detects after configuration occurs, not firewall to block previous action.

7. Notification

Khi resource NON_COMPLIANT:

AWS Config
↓
EventBridge / SNS
↓
Alert
Security Group opens port 22
↓
Config = NON_COMPLIANT
↓
EventBridge
↓
SNS
↓
Security Team

8. Auto Remediation

Config can automatically edit resources through SSM Automation.

NON_COMPLIANT
↓
AWS Config
↓
SSM Automation
↓
Fix configuration
IAM Access Key expired
↓
Config
↓
NON_COMPLIANT
↓
SSM Automation
↓
Revoke Key

Remediation requires appropriate IAM permission.

9. Multi-Account / Multi-Region

AWS Config works by Region but can be aggregated:

Account A / Region A
Account B / Region B
Account C / Region C
        ↓
Config Aggregator
        ↓
Central Security Account

Great for managing compliance for multiple AWS accounts.

10. S3 + Athena

Config data can be saved to S3:

AWS Config
↓
S3
↓
Athena
↓
SQL Analysis / Report

Used for long-term audit.

11. CloudWatch vs CloudTrail vs Config

  • CloudWatch = How does the system run?
  • CPU = 95% · High Latency · Many errors
  • CloudTrail = who did what?

Who edits Security Group? · Who deletes EC2? · Which API is called?

AWS Config = how is resource configured? = is it compliant?

Does Security Group open port 22? · Is S3 public? · Does ALB have SSL certificate?

12. Combined example

Assuming Security Group is opened:

0.0.0.0/0 → Port 22

12.1. CloudTrail

Who fixed SG?
↓
User A
AuthorizeSecurityGroupIngress

12.2. AWS Config

Current SG:
Port 22 opens to the Internet
↓
NON_COMPLIANT

12.3. CloudWatch

Monitor application/server metrics · CPU / Logs / Errors
